<h2 id="anthropic--claude-opus-5-5">1. claude-opus-5-5 · Anthropic API</h2>

<p><strong>90</strong>/100: understanding 36/40 · accuracy 27/30 · accessibility 19/20 · calibration 8/10<br>606 words · 1,400 output tokens (286 reported as reasoning) · effort: adaptive, medium</p>

<div class="explainer-flag"><p><strong>Model errors about a real person</strong>: claims the thread does not support.</p><ul><li>“one of his programs crashed”, “a mistake in his code”: the thread never gives the reporter's gender.</li></ul></div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

**What went wrong**

Rust is a programming language, and a compiler is the program that translates human-written Rust code into instructions a computer can execute. In August 2026 the maintainer of Rama, a networking toolkit, found that after upgrading to Rust version 1.98, one of his programs crashed. The same code worked with the previous version, 1.97.1, and with a slightly newer experimental build. The crash was not caused by a mistake in his code. The compiler itself had produced faulty instructions.

To see how, consider how programs handle flexibility. Sometimes code needs to call "the serve action of whatever service this is" without knowing in advance which kind of service it will be. To make this work, the compiler builds a small directory for each kind of service, called a *vtable*. Each entry in the directory is the address where a particular action's instructions live. At runtime the program looks up the right entry and jumps to that address.

In Rama's case, one directory had a blank entry: the address was simply zero. When the program looked up "serve" and jumped there, it jumped to nowhere, and the operating system killed it. Rust promises that code written in its ordinary, safe style can never do this, so this was a serious bug.

**Why it happened**

The compiler sometimes legitimately leaves directory entries blank. If it can prove that an action could never actually be called, because the conditions it requires can never all be true at once, there is no point generating it. That proof is called the "impossible predicates" check. Here the compiler wrongly concluded that the conditions for Rama's serve action were impossible, so it skipped generating it and left a hole.

The reasoning behind that check is done by a component called the *trait solver*, which works out whether types satisfy given requirements. Rust is gradually replacing its old solver with a new one. Most of the language still uses the old solver, but the impossible-predicates check already uses the new one. That meant bugs in the "experimental" solver could reach ordinary users.

In June, a change to the new solver added a shortcut: marking certain type expressions as fully worked out ("rigid") so they would not be recalculated repeatedly. This exposed an existing gap. When the solver meets certain *hidden* types, such as the return type of an asynchronous function, it is supposed to redo its reasoning in a mode that can see what those types really are. In one combination of circumstances, it declined to redo the work and simply reported failure. That failure was read as "impossible," and the entry was left blank. Volunteers shrank Rama's huge program down to about 40 lines that reproduced the crash. They also pinpointed the exact change that caused it.

**How it was fixed**

A developer had already found and fixed the gap in July, while chasing a different crash. The fix is literally a one-word change, from "No" to "Yes": the solver now redoes its reasoning in that combination too. However, Rust releases are cut from a branch every six weeks. The bug slipped in just before the cut for 1.98, and the fix landed just after. So the fix reached the next version, 1.99, while 1.98 shipped broken.

Someone also demonstrated that the danger went beyond crashes. With optimizations turned on, the compiler assumes the blank entry is valid and may rearrange code in ways that silently change a program's behavior. Given that, the Rust team issued an emergency point release, 1.98.1, containing the fix plus a test to prevent recurrence. It shipped on September 3, and the issue was closed.

</div>

<h2 id="codex--gpt-6-sol">2. gpt-6-sol · ChatGPT sub</h2>

<p><strong>88</strong>/100: understanding 34/40 · accuracy 29/30 · accessibility 16/20 · calibration 9/10<br>423 words · 571 output tokens (49 reported as reasoning) · effort: medium*</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust lets a program call a method without knowing the exact kind of object receiving the call. To do that, the compiler builds a small table of addresses—called a *vtable*—that tells the program where each method’s code lives. In this bug, Rust 1.98.0 built a table with a zero where a method address should have been. When a proxy server written in safe Rust called that method, it jumped to address zero and crashed.

The proxy was supposed to handle a failed connection by returning “502 Bad Gateway” and continuing to run. That is what happened with Rust 1.97.1 and with a later nightly compiler. With 1.98.0, the same source code could instead crash while handling the request. The reporter traced the crash to a boxed asynchronous service: a component whose precise type had been hidden so it could be called through the vtable. The compiler had generated the caller, but had wrongly left out the callable method’s entry.

Why would it leave a method out? Before putting a method in a vtable, the compiler checks whether the conditions required for that method could possibly be satisfied. If it concludes they cannot, it may leave the slot vacant. Here, that conclusion was wrong. The conditions involved an *opaque type*: a type whose concrete identity is deliberately hidden at one stage of compilation but is available at another. Rust’s trait solver—the part of the compiler that reasons about which types meet which requirements—needed to reconsider its answer using that later information.

Two compiler changes had landed on opposite sides of the cutoff for the 1.98 release. The first changed how the solver handled certain type aliases and exposed this case. The second corrected when the solver repeats a check in its original typing mode after encountering opaque types. Without the correction, two reasons for repeating the check could be combined in a way that made the solver skip the repeat. It then treated a usable method as impossible, producing the empty vtable slot.

This was a compiler bug, not misuse of an unsafe feature by the application. Although the most obvious result was a crash, an invalid method address makes the program’s behavior undefined; investigators also found an optimized example that changed behavior without simply crashing.

The community reduced the large proxy example to a short, self-contained reproduction and identified the relevant compiler changes. Rust’s maintainers backported the solver correction, added a regression test that actually calls through the affected vtable, and released it in **Rust 1.98.1**. The issue was closed after that release.

</div>

<div class="explainer-flag"><p><strong>Model errors about a real person</strong>: claims the thread does not support.</p><ul><li>“a developer named Glen”: the thread names the reporter only by the GitHub handle GlenDC.</li><li>“his networking software (called Rama)”: the thread never gives the reporter's gender.</li></ul></div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

# The Vacant Vtable Bug in Rust 1.98

## What Happened

In August 2026, a developer named Glen noticed that his networking software (called Rama) started crashing after updating to Rust version 1.98. The program would die with a "segmentation fault" — essentially, it tried to jump to memory address zero, which is always invalid. The same code worked perfectly on the previous version (1.97.1) and on newer development versions. Something specific to version 1.98 was generating broken programs.

## Understanding the Mechanism

To understand the bug, you need to know about a technique called "dynamic dispatch." Imagine you have a general category — say "vehicle" — and specific types like "car" and "bicycle." When your program handles a generic "vehicle" without knowing which specific type it is, it needs a lookup table (called a "vtable") that says: "if you want to call the 'drive' method, jump to this memory address." Each specific type gets its own vtable with the correct addresses filled in.

The bug caused the compiler to leave one of these vtable entries empty — filled with zero instead of a valid address. When the program later tried to call that method through the lookup table, it followed the zero address, which is like being given directions to a house that doesn't exist. The processor crashed immediately.

## Why It Happened

The root cause involved two changes to Rust's internals that shipped at different times, creating a dangerous gap.

Rust has a component called the "trait solver" — the part of the compiler that figures out whether types satisfy certain requirements. A new, more powerful version of this solver was being gradually introduced. Even though the new solver wasn't fully enabled for regular code, it was already being used behind the scenes for a specific optimization called `impossible_predicates`. This optimization asks: "Could this combination of types ever actually exist?" If the answer is no, the compiler skips generating code for it, leaving the vtable slot empty.

The first change (PR #156742, merged in June) added a feature called "rigid alias markers" to help the new solver track type information more efficiently. However, this introduced a subtle flaw: in certain situations involving opaque types (types whose internal structure is hidden, like the return type of `async` functions), the solver would incorrectly conclude that a perfectly valid type combination was impossible. It would then skip generating the method code, leaving a zero in the vtable.

The second change (PR #158993, merged in July) fixed this flaw by ensuring the solver would re-examine its conclusions in the right context when it encountered these opaque types. Unfortunately, the first change shipped in Rust 1.98 (released August 20), while the fix missed that release and only landed in the next development cycle. This created a window where stable Rust produced silently broken programs.

## The Fix

The fix was straightforward: the second change was backported to create Rust 1.98.1, released on September 3. The actual code change was remarkably small — changing a single line from `RerunDecision::No` to `RerunDecision::Yes` — telling the solver to retry its analysis in the correct mode rather than giving up and wrongly declaring the type combination impossible.

## The Broader Lesson

This incident highlighted a risk in gradually deploying new compiler internals: even features not directly visible to users can affect program correctness through indirect pathways like optimization decisions. The Rust team noted they needed to be more careful when changes to the new solver straddled release boundaries.

</div>

<h2 id="anthropic--claude-fable-5">4. claude-fable-5 · Anthropic API</h2>

<p><strong>87</strong>/100: understanding 35/40 · accuracy 26/30 · accessibility 18/20 · calibration 8/10<br>585 words · 1,264 output tokens (132 reported as reasoning) · effort: adaptive, high</p>

<div class="explainer-flag"><p><strong>Model errors about a real person</strong>: claims the thread does not support.</p><ul><li>“the first compiler bug he'd hit”: the reporter called it the first stable rustc bug they had hit, not the first compiler bug, and the thread never gives the reporter's gender.</li></ul></div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

**What happened, in plain terms**

The Rust programming language comes with a compiler — a program that translates human-written code into machine instructions. Rust's big selling point is safety: if your code compiles, it's supposed to be free of a whole class of dangerous errors, like accidentally jumping to a nonsense location in memory. This bug broke that promise. The compiler itself made a mistake, so a perfectly correct program crashed when it ran.

**The lookup table with a hole in it**

Some programs need to decide *while running* which piece of code to execute — think of a switchboard operator connecting calls to different departments. To make this work, the compiler builds a small lookup table (called a "vtable") listing the memory addresses of the relevant functions. When the program needs one of those functions, it reads the address from the table and jumps there.

As an optimization, the compiler checks whether each function in the table could ever actually be called. If the compiler can prove a function's requirements are *impossible* to satisfy, it leaves that slot empty — writing a zero instead of a real address — since, in theory, nothing will ever look it up.

Here, the compiler's reasoning engine got that proof wrong. It concluded that a genuinely usable function was "impossible," and left a zero in its table slot. When the program — a network proxy called Rama — later tried to call that function, it jumped to memory address zero, which is invalid, and the operating system killed it instantly ("segmentation fault"). Worse, as commenters demonstrated, the optimizer is allowed to *assume* such situations never happen, so in optimized builds the bug could silently produce wrong behavior rather than an honest crash.

**Why it happened**

Rust's developers are gradually replacing the compiler's "trait solver" — the logical reasoning engine that answers questions like "does this type satisfy these requirements?" Although the new solver isn't fully switched on yet, a few compiler tasks already use it, including the very check that decides whether a vtable slot can be left empty.

A large internal change to the new solver (an efficiency improvement) introduced a subtle logic error: in one specific, convoluted situation — involving a function whose exact return type is hidden behind an "opaque" placeholder (common with Rust's `async` functions) — the solver gave up and declared the requirements unsatisfiable instead of re-checking with fuller information. The mistake was actually noticed and fixed a couple of weeks later. Unfortunately, Rust releases are cut from the codebase on a fixed schedule, and the cutoff for version 1.98 fell *between* the buggy change and its fix. So 1.98 shipped with the bug, while both the previous version (1.97.1) and later nightly builds were fine — exactly the confusing pattern the bug reporter observed.

**How it was resolved**

The reporter, whose automated tests caught the crash the moment 1.98 released, filed a detailed report. Volunteers whittled the enormous real-world project down to a ~30-line example, then used automated bisection tools to pinpoint both the commit that broke things and the one that had already fixed them. The one-line fix — essentially changing a "don't re-check" into a "do re-check" — was backported, and Rust 1.98.1 was released about two weeks later.

The episode ended constructively: the reporter noted this was the first compiler bug he'd hit in eleven years of production Rust, and added daily testing against Rust's pre-release versions to help catch any future problems before they reach a stable release.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-6-sol">5. gpt-6-sol · OpenAI API</h2>

<p><strong>87</strong>/100: understanding 34/40 · accuracy 26/30 · accessibility 18/20 · calibration 9/10<br>477 words · 629 output tokens (52 reported as reasoning) · effort: medium</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

A Rust program can call a method without knowing the exact type of the value receiving the call. To make that work, the compiler builds a **vtable**: a small table containing, among other things, the address of the method’s code. At the moment of the call, the program looks up that address and jumps to it.

In Rust 1.98.0, the compiler sometimes put a zero where a method address belonged. The Rama networking project encountered this while handling a request through a boxed asynchronous service—a component whose exact type had been hidden behind a common interface. When the program called the service, it jumped to address zero and crashed. The same source code worked with Rust 1.97.1 and with a later nightly compiler. This was a compiler error, not a mistake in Rama’s safe Rust code.

Why would the compiler leave a method slot empty? It can do so when it has proved that the method cannot possibly be called for that type. Such a slot is called *vacant*. In this case, the proof was wrong: the service was valid, and the program really could call it. Because the compiler believed the method was impossible, it also failed to generate the concrete method code needed by the vtable.

The faulty proof arose in Rust’s machinery for reasoning about types. Some types, including the result types of certain functions, are deliberately left unspecified in source code; the compiler works out what they mean in context. Rust 1.98 gained a change to how this machinery handles such types, but a related correction missed the release cutoff. During a later compilation stage, the type checker could reach a conclusion using an incomplete view of one of these hidden types. In certain circumstances, it should have retried the check in the original context. Instead, it treated the failed check as final, allowing the compiler to mark a callable method as impossible.

Although the underlying bug was in newer type-reasoning machinery, it reached ordinary stable Rust builds because one check used while generating vtables already relied on that machinery. That made this more than a problem confined to an experimental compiler option.

Contributors reduced Rama’s large networking example to a tiny program that still triggered the error, then traced its introduction to the earlier compiler change. They identified the missing correction, which had already landed for a subsequent Rust release. Rust 1.98.1 backported it: when the compiler encounters the relevant hidden types during these later checks, it reruns the reasoning in the proper context. The patch also added a regression test that calls a method through a vtable, so a vacant slot in this situation will be caught.

The most visible result was a crash, but an incorrect method address can have other effects once optimizations are involved. The reliable fix was therefore in the compiler, rather than a workaround in Rama.

</div>

<h2 id="anthropic--claude-fable-5-1">6. claude-fable-5-1 · Anthropic API</h2>

<p><strong>87</strong>/100: understanding 35/40 · accuracy 25/30 · accessibility 19/20 · calibration 8/10<br>596 words · 1,236 output tokens (193 reported as reasoning) · effort: adaptive, high</p>

<div class="explainer-flag"><p><strong>Model errors about a real person</strong>: claims the thread does not support.</p><ul><li>“the moment he rebuilt it”, “Nothing in his own code had changed”: the thread never gives the reporter's gender.</li></ul></div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

In August 2026, a Rust developer noticed that a networking program which had worked fine for years suddenly began crashing the moment he rebuilt it with the newest version of the Rust compiler (1.98). Nothing in his own code had changed. The crash was severe: the program tried to jump to memory address zero, which is like being told to go to a house at "address nothing." The operating system killed it instantly.

To understand what went wrong, it helps to know one idea from how compiled programs work. Sometimes a program deals with an object whose exact type isn't known until the program is running. To handle this, the compiler builds a small lookup table for each such object, a kind of directory listing where each of the object's methods lives in memory. When the program needs to call a method, it looks up the entry in the directory and jumps there. In this bug, the compiler had filled in most of the directory correctly, but left one entry blank, a zero. When the program looked up that method and jumped, it jumped to nowhere.

Why would a compiler leave an entry blank on purpose? Rust's compiler has a legitimate feature for this. Some methods come with conditions attached ("this only works if the input type can be sorted," say), and if the compiler can prove those conditions can *never* be satisfied for a particular type, it knows the method can never be called. Rather than generate useless code, it leaves the slot empty. This is safe as long as the compiler's proof is correct.

The proof was wrong. Deciding whether conditions can be satisfied is the job of a component called the trait solver, and Rust has been gradually replacing its old solver with a new one. In May 2026, a large change to the new solver was merged (adding "rigidness markers," an internal optimization about when the compiler can trust it has fully simplified a type). That change introduced a subtle flaw: in a specific late stage of compilation, when the solver encountered a certain kind of placeholder type (produced by asynchronous functions, among other things), it was supposed to re-check its reasoning under different rules, but a decision table said "no, don't re-check." Skipping that step caused the solver to wrongly conclude that the method's conditions were impossible. So it left the slot blank, and it did so silently, without the error message a compiler normally produces when confused.

The flaw was actually discovered and fixed in July, about two weeks after it was introduced, by a developer who hit an internal compiler crash while building a different project. The fix was tiny: one word in a decision table changed from "No" to "Yes." But Rust releases work like trains on a schedule. The bug had boarded the train for version 1.98 before the fix arrived, and nobody noticed that the two were separated by that cutoff. So 1.98 shipped with the bug but not the fix, while the following version had both.

Once the report came in, volunteers narrowed the crash from a large networking library down to a 30-line example, pinpointed the exact commit that caused it, and confirmed the July fix cured it. The Rust team judged this serious enough for an emergency patch release. Version 1.98.1, containing the one-word fix and two new regression tests, shipped on September 3, two weeks after the report. The reporter added a daily test run against pre-release compilers so future problems of this kind could be caught before they reach the public.

</div>

<h2 id="codex--gpt-6-astra">7. gpt-6-astra · ChatGPT sub</h2>

<p><strong>86</strong>/100: understanding 34/40 · accuracy 28/30 · accessibility 16/20 · calibration 8/10<br>576 words · 696 output tokens · effort: medium*</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

This was a bug in Rust’s compiler, the tool that translates Rust source code into an executable program. The application’s code was valid, but Rust 1.98.0 translated part of it incorrectly. That matters especially for Rust: its “safe” programming features are designed to prevent certain dangerous memory errors, but those guarantees depend on the compiler doing its job correctly.

The problem surfaced in Rama, a networking project. A proxy server was supposed to try connecting to another server and, if that failed, return an ordinary “502 Bad Gateway” response. Instead, after being built with Rust 1.98.0, it crashed. The same source code worked with the previous release and with a newer development version.

### What went wrong

Programs sometimes need to choose which operation to perform at runtime rather than deciding everything in advance. Rust supports this using a compiler-generated table of addresses called a “vtable.” Think of it as a directory: to perform a particular service’s operation, the program looks up the corresponding address and jumps there.

In this case, the directory contained a zero where a valid operation’s address should have been. The running program trusted the directory, looked up that entry, and tried to execute instructions at address zero. On the reporter’s Mac, that produced an immediate crash.

The empty entry was not random memory corruption. The compiler itself had deliberately left the slot vacant because it had incorrectly concluded that the operation’s requirements could never be satisfied.

### Why it happened

Rust’s compiler contains a reasoning system that checks whether types meet various requirements. For example, it may need to establish that a particular service produces a particular kind of response. These checks can involve several layers of relationships.

A recent compiler change introduced a marker that lets this reasoning system avoid repeatedly simplifying certain type expressions. That shortcut depends on the context in which the compiler is reasoning. A conclusion appropriate in one context may need reconsideration in another.

The failing case involved “opaque” types: types whose exact identity is intentionally hidden behind a description of what they can do. Asynchronous functions introduce such types, which initially made this look like an async-specific problem. Contributors reduced the large networking application to a tiny example and showed that an ordinary function returning a hidden type could trigger it too.

The reasoning system already knew that some encounters with opaque types required retrying a check in the original context. But one combined retry condition was mistakenly treated as “do not retry” during later compilation stages. A check therefore failed when it should have succeeded. That false failure reached the code responsible for deciding whether vtable entries were usable.

Although the fault was in Rust’s newer type-reasoning machinery, it affected stable Rust because the compiler already used that machinery for certain checks, including this one.

### How it was fixed

An existing development-branch fix changed the relevant decision from “do not retry” to “retry.” It had originally addressed a different compiler failure, but it corrected the underlying mistake here as well. A release-branch cutoff had separated the change that introduced the regression from its fix.

The maintainers backported the fix and added a regression test that actually runs the small triggering program. Rust 1.98.1 shipped with the correction, and the issue was closed.

The urgency went beyond crashes: with optimization enabled, contributors demonstrated altered program behavior too. This was a genuine compiler soundness bug, not an application mistake or merely an inconvenient error message.

</div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust 1.98 contained a compiler bug that could turn entirely safe Rust code into a broken executable. The Rama web-proxy project happened to exercise it through a complicated combination of generic components, dynamically selected services, and hidden return types.

To understand the failure, imagine that an object carries a small directory listing the operations it supports. Rust calls this directory a “virtual method table,” or vtable. Each entry contains the address of the machine code for one operation. When a program asks an object to perform an operation without knowing its exact concrete type, it looks up the address in this table and jumps to it.

For the affected service, Rust 1.98 generated a table whose ordinary bookkeeping entries—such as the object’s size and alignment—were correct. But the first operation’s entry was zero instead of the address of the service method. When Rama tried to call that method, the processor jumped to address zero. This normally caused an immediate segmentation fault, although optimized builds could exhibit other behavior. A null function address creates “undefined behavior,” meaning the optimizer is permitted to make assumptions that can produce results stranger than a straightforward crash.

The compiler left the slot empty because it incorrectly concluded that the method’s implementation could never be valid or reachable. Rust’s type system must prove many conditions when assembling such a table: for example, that associated input and output types agree and that all required capabilities are present. Some types are deliberately hidden behind phrases equivalent to “some type satisfying these conditions.” Internally, these are called opaque types.

A change merged before Rust 1.98 introduced a new way to mark certain unresolved type expressions as fixed, or “rigid,” within a particular type-checking context. This was intended to avoid unnecessary repeated work. However, those markings are valid only in the context where they were created. During the later code-generation stage, the compiler’s newer trait-solving machinery encountered opaque types while checking whether the vtable method was possible. Under one combined set of circumstances, it failed to repeat the check in the original, appropriate type-checking mode. The resulting false failure made the compiler classify the method as impossible and emit a vacant vtable entry.

This explains the peculiar release pattern. Rust 1.97 predated the change and worked. Rust 1.98 included the change but not its follow-up correction, so it miscompiled the program. Later nightly builds and Rust 1.99 beta already contained that correction. A beta-release cutoff had accidentally fallen between the two related changes.

The fix was small but important: when the solver encounters the relevant combination of opaque-type conditions during post-analysis or code generation, it must rerun the evaluation in the original typing mode rather than accepting the apparent failure. That allows it to recognize the implementation as valid and place the real method address in the vtable.

The correction was backported to a special Rust 1.98.1 patch release. The developers also added a compact regression test that constructs the problematic dynamic call and verifies that it runs successfully. The issue was therefore a genuine compiler miscompilation—not misuse by Rama—and upgrading from 1.98.0 to 1.98.1 fixes it.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.5-2026-04-23">9. gpt-5.5-2026-04-23 · OpenAI API</h2>

<p><strong>84</strong>/100: understanding 33/40 · accuracy 28/30 · accessibility 15/20 · calibration 8/10<br>652 words · 992 output tokens (93 reported as reasoning) · effort: medium</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

This bug was a Rust compiler miscompilation: a correct Rust program was translated into an incorrect machine-code program. The source code itself did not contain unsafe code causing the crash. Instead, Rust 1.98 generated a broken internal dispatch table, and the resulting program jumped to memory address zero, which caused a segmentation fault.

The affected program used a common Rust pattern: “boxed” dynamic services. In plain terms, the program had a value that could be one of several concrete service implementations, but it was stored behind a uniform interface. This is like having a remote control labeled “play” without knowing whether it controls a TV, stereo, or projector. At runtime, Rust uses a hidden table, called a vtable, to know which actual function should be called when the program says “run this service.”

A vtable is essentially a list of function addresses. For example: how to destroy the object, how large it is, how it is aligned in memory, and where its methods live. In this bug, Rust 1.98 emitted a vtable where the basic housekeeping entries were valid, but the first real method entry was zero. So when safe Rust code tried to call the service method through that table, the compiled program loaded “call address 0” and jumped there. Address zero is not a valid function, so the process crashed.

The especially serious part is that this happened in safe Rust. Rust’s safety promises depend on the compiler correctly enforcing and preserving type information. A null method slot in a vtable is not supposed to be possible for a callable trait object. Once the compiler produces such code, the program has undefined behavior: it might crash, behave strangely, or appear to work depending on optimization level and circumstances.

The root cause was in Rust’s trait-solving machinery. Rust traits are interface-like constraints: “this type can do X,” “this output type satisfies Y,” and so on. The compiler must prove these constraints while compiling. In this case, the compiler was analyzing whether a particular trait method could ever be called for a complicated generic type involving async functions, hidden return types, and associated type constraints.

Rust 1.98 contained a change related to “rigid aliases,” an internal optimization in the newer trait solver. That change was intended to avoid repeatedly normalizing some type aliases. However, in a later compilation phase, the compiler sometimes needed to reconsider hidden or “opaque” types — types written as “some type implementing this property,” such as those produced by async functions or `impl Trait`. Because of the bug, the compiler failed to rerun a trait check in the correct mode when such opaque types appeared. It incorrectly concluded that some method predicates were impossible.

That mistaken conclusion mattered during vtable construction. If the compiler thinks a trait method can never be valid for a particular implementation, it may mark that vtable slot as vacant. But in this case the method was valid and was later called. The compiler had made an impossible state possible: a callable object whose method pointer was missing.

The bug was introduced by Rust pull request #156742 and released in Rust 1.98. It had already been fixed on nightly by pull request #158993, but that fix missed the 1.98 release cutoff. Once the bug report identified the stable regression, the Rust team backported the fix in Rust 1.98.1.

The fix was small but important: when the trait solver encounters certain opaque-type conditions during later compiler phases such as code generation, it now reruns the evaluation in the original typing mode instead of trusting the earlier failed result. In simpler terms, the compiler now double-checks the “this method is impossible” conclusion in the context where hidden types can be interpreted correctly.

Rust 1.98.1 also added regression tests, including a minimized version of the reported case, to ensure future compilers do not again generate a vtable with a missing method entry for a valid trait object.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.2">10. gpt-5.2 · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

A Rust user hit a rare but serious compiler bug in Rust 1.98: a program written in *safe* Rust (code that is supposed to be protected from memory-safety crashes) sometimes crashed at runtime with a segmentation fault. The crash happened when the program tried to call a “boxed” service—roughly, an object stored behind a generic “handle” that lets you call different implementations through the same interface.

Under the hood, when Rust uses this kind of “call through an interface,” it relies on a small table of function pointers, called a *vtable* (virtual method table). You can think of a vtable like a remote control: pressing button 1 calls the correct function for that particular device. The vtable must contain valid addresses for each method. In Rust 1.98, the compiler sometimes generated a vtable where one of those method entries was *zero* (a null pointer). Then, when the program pressed that button (called the method), the CPU tried to jump to address 0—an invalid address—causing an immediate crash (SIGSEGV). In other minimized examples the same flaw could produce other “anything can happen” behavior, because once you jump to nonsense code, the machine is no longer executing the intended program.

Why would a compiler ever put “empty” into a vtable? The key is that Rust’s compiler aggressively proves which code paths are possible. If it concludes that a certain method can never be called for a particular trait object (because its requirements can never be satisfied), it may treat that method as unreachable and omit generating a real function pointer, leaving a “vacant” slot. Normally, that’s safe only if the method truly is impossible to call.

Here, the compiler’s reasoning went wrong. The issue was triggered by a complicated mix of generics, type constraints, and “hidden” types produced by `async` functions (and similar constructs that return opaque, unnamed types). In Rust 1.98, a change related to the “next trait solver” (an internal engine for answering “does this type satisfy this requirement?”) affected a check called “impossible predicates.” During a late compilation phase (post-analysis/code generation), the solver sometimes evaluated these requirements in the wrong “typing mode,” making it incorrectly decide that certain requirements were impossible. That mistaken conclusion then caused the vtable entry to be left null.

The fix was not to change user code, but to correct the compiler’s bookkeeping: if, during late compilation, the solver encounters these opaque/hidden types under certain conditions, it must *rerun* the evaluation using the original typing mode where the opaque types are handled correctly. A Rust PR (#158993) implemented this rerun rule more completely. Although it first landed on nightly/beta, it missed the Rust 1.98 release cutoff, so stable 1.98 shipped with the bug. The Rust team then backported the fix into a patch release, Rust 1.98.1 (#161555), and added tests to ensure vtables are no longer generated with bogus vacant method slots in this scenario.

</div>

<h2 id="moonshot--kimi-k3">11. kimi-k3 · Moonshot API</h2>

<p><strong>83</strong>/100: understanding 34/40 · accuracy 24/30 · accessibility 18/20 · calibration 7/10<br>518 words · 3,146 output tokens (2,469 reported as reasoning) · effort: thinking on</p>

<div class="explainer-flag"><p><strong>Model errors about a real person</strong>: claims the thread does not support.</p><ul><li>“he opened his report by insisting he was probably doing something wrong himself”, “He wasn't.”, “His program, a web proxy”, “daily testing of his project”: the thread never gives the reporter's gender.</li></ul></div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust is a programming language whose compiler—the tool that translates human-written code into a running program—is famously fussy and reliable. So when an eleven-year veteran hit this bug, he opened his report by insisting he was probably doing something wrong himself. He wasn't.

His program, a web proxy, crashed the instant it received a particular kind of request—but only when built with Rust version 1.98. Identical source code worked fine on version 1.97 and on newer experimental builds. The crash report showed the program counter at address zero: the program had tried to jump to an instruction located at "memory address 0," like a GPS sending you to house number zero on a street that doesn't exist. Everything halts.

Understanding why requires two ideas. First, "dynamic dispatch": Rust programmers can hand around components whose exact type isn't known in advance, so long as they promise certain abilities ("I can serve a request"). The compiler builds a small table for each—a vtable—listing where each promised ability actually lives in memory, like a receptionist's contact sheet. Second, the compiler contains an automated reasoning engine that constantly proves claims like "yes, this type genuinely satisfies these requirements."

The bug lived in that reasoning engine—specifically an experimental next-generation one, not yet officially enabled, but already used behind the scenes, including for the check that decides whether a method's requirements are flat-out impossible. Months earlier, an improvement (change #156742) taught the engine to treat certain type nicknames as "rigid," temporarily forbidding itself from peeking inside them. That change had a subtle flaw involving "opaque types," a feature where a function deliberately hides its exact return type. In the reasoning mode used while generating final machine code, the engine was supposed to quietly redo a proof under different rules whenever such hidden types were involved. It didn't. It compared a rigid nickname against the now-revealed underlying type, concluded "these can never match," and decided the method's requirements could never be satisfied—so no one could ever legitimately call it. The compiler therefore left that method's slot in the vtable blank: a literal zero, next to perfectly valid bookkeeping entries. Ordinary, safe Rust code then dialed that number, jumped to nowhere, and the operating system killed the process.

The cruel part is that the flaw had already been fixed on the development branch by change #158993—days after the 1.98 release branched off. The stable release shipped with the bug but not the repair, a pure timing accident.

Once reported, the community moved fast. Volunteers bisected thousands of compiler versions to find both the offending change and the prior fix, and shrank the crashing proxy into a self-contained program of about forty lines—small enough that the diagnosis became undeniable. The actual repair was almost comically tiny: changing one word in the compiler from "No" to "Yes," telling the engine, *yes, re-run the proof in this situation*. That one-word backport shipped as Rust 1.98.1 two weeks after the report. The reporter, meanwhile, added daily testing of his project against pre-release compilers—so the next time a release branches at an unlucky moment, someone will notice before it reaches users.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-6-astra">12. gpt-6-astra · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust’s safety guarantees depend on its compiler translating valid programs into correct machine instructions. This bug broke that assumption: Rust 1.98.0 could turn safe code into a program that tried to execute instructions at memory address zero. The problem was in the compiler, not in the application’s use of Rust.

### What went wrong

The bug surfaced in Rama, a networking toolkit. When its proxy could not connect to an upstream server, it should have returned an HTTP “502 Bad Gateway” response and continued running. Instead, a particular request crashed the proxy.

The failure involved how a program calls an operation when the exact kind of object providing it is not known until the program runs. Rust supports this through a compiler-generated lookup table called a *vtable*. Think of it as a directory listing which address to visit for each operation.

In this case, the directory existed, and its basic information looked valid. But the entry for an operation the program needed was zero—effectively a blank address. The generated instructions looked up that entry and jumped there, causing the crash.

The same application worked with Rust 1.97.1 and with a later development version. Investigators also reproduced the regression on Linux, showing that it was not just a macOS problem.

### Why it happened

Rust’s compiler checks many conditions about types: whether an object supports an operation, whether its output meets certain requirements, and so on. A component called the *trait solver* answers these questions.

When constructing a vtable, the compiler can deliberately leave an entry vacant if it concludes that the operation’s requirements are impossible to satisfy. That is legitimate when the operation truly cannot be called. Here, however, the solver incorrectly declared a valid operation impossible.

The underlying mistake concerned *opaque types*: types whose exact identity is hidden behind a promise about what they can do. These arise naturally from asynchronous functions, but investigators eventually showed that asynchronous execution itself was not necessary to trigger the bug.

The compiler reasons about these hidden types differently at different stages. An earlier change introduced markers allowing it to avoid repeatedly resolving certain type expressions. Those shortcuts depend on the reasoning context remaining appropriate.

For some questions involving opaque types, the solver needed to repeat its evaluation in the original typing mode. It already did so for one triggering condition, but not for a combination of related conditions. That missing retry allowed a failed evaluation to become the wrong final answer: “this operation is impossible.”

Although this involved the newer trait solver, ordinary stable Rust was affected because vtable-related checks already used it.

### How it was fixed

The missing retry had actually been repaired during development before this report. Unfortunately, a release-branch cutoff separated the change that introduced the regression from its fix. Rust 1.98.0 therefore shipped with the problem, while later development builds worked.

The repair made the combined condition request another evaluation during the affected compiler stages. Maintainers brought that change into the stable branch and added a regression test that actually runs a small program and calls the affected operation.

Rust 1.98.1 shipped the fix on September 3, 2026. This mattered beyond preventing crashes: the invalid call created undefined behavior, allowing optimization to produce other incorrect results too. Fixing the compiler restored the missing valid operation rather than merely masking one symptom.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.5-pro-2026-04-23">13. gpt-5.5-pro-2026-04-23 · OpenAI API</h2>

<p><strong>82</strong>/100: understanding 33/40 · accuracy 26/30 · accessibility 15/20 · calibration 8/10<br>535 words · 3,103 output tokens (1,206 reported as reasoning) · effort: high</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust 1.98 briefly broke one of Rust’s core promises: ordinary safe Rust code should not be able to crash by jumping to address zero.

The affected project, Rama, used a common Rust pattern: store “some service” behind a box, then call it later without knowing its exact concrete type. To make that work, Rust builds a hidden table beside the boxed value. This table, called a vtable, is like a small directory of function addresses: “to drop this value, call here; to get its size, use this; to call method `serve`, jump there.”

In Rust 1.98, for one particular kind of boxed async service, the compiler generated a vtable where the housekeeping entries were valid, but the first method entry was zero. Later, safe Rust code performed dynamic dispatch: it looked up the method address in the table and jumped to it. Since the address was zero, the program segfaulted. In optimized builds, this could also lead to stranger behavior, because once a compiler emits an impossible-looking program state, the machine code optimizer may make assumptions that no longer hold.

Why did the compiler put a zero there? Rust has a concept of “vacant” vtable slots. They are used when a method can never actually be called for a particular type because its requirements are impossible to satisfy. That is normally fine. The bug was that rustc incorrectly decided that a real, callable method was impossible.

The wrong decision came from Rust’s trait-solving machinery. Traits are Rust’s way of saying “this type supports this behavior.” For generic code, the compiler must prove many logical facts: this type implements that trait, this associated type equals that other type, this method’s requirements hold, and so on.

Rust 1.98 included a change related to “rigid aliases,” an internal optimization for handling type aliases and opaque types. Opaque types are types whose real identity is known to the compiler but hidden in source code—`async fn` is a major source of them, because every async function returns a hidden future type. The minimized reproducer showed that `async` was not magical by itself; other hidden `impl Trait`-style types could also trigger the issue.

The compiler uses different “typing modes” at different phases. Earlier phases may treat an opaque type as hidden; later code-generation phases often need to reveal what it really is. The new solver was supposed to rerun certain checks in the original, appropriate mode when it encountered opaque types. But one combined condition failed to request that rerun during post-analysis/codegen. As a result, the solver looked at a still-hidden type in the wrong mode, failed to prove a valid requirement, and told the vtable builder the method was impossible.

The fix was small but important: change that rerun decision from “no” to “yes” for the affected condition. With the check rerun in the right mode, the compiler sees the real hidden type, proves the method is valid, generates the method body, and writes the correct function address into the vtable.

The fix had already landed on nightly after the 1.98 cutoff, which is why older Rust worked, 1.98.0 failed, and later nightly/beta worked again. It was backported in Rust 1.98.1, along with regression tests based on the minimized example.

</div>

This was a compiler bug, not a bug in the user’s program.

At a high level, the program was using a common Rust pattern: store “something that can handle a request” behind a generic interface, then call it later. Rust implements that kind of runtime polymorphism with a **vtable**: a little table of function pointers saying “if you ask this object to do method X, jump to this machine-code address.”

In Rust 1.98, the compiler sometimes built that table incorrectly. It filled in the housekeeping fields of the table correctly, but left the actual method entry as **zero** — essentially a null function pointer. Later, perfectly safe Rust code looked up that method in the vtable and jumped to it. Jumping to address 0 is invalid, so the process crashed with a segmentation fault. In other cases, because this is undefined behavior, it could do stranger things too.

Why did the compiler think it was okay to leave the slot empty? Because, during compilation, it wrongly convinced itself that the method could never actually exist for that concrete type.

The mistake lived in Rust’s **trait solver**, the part of the compiler that answers questions like “does this type implement this interface under these conditions?” This particular bug involved a tricky combination of:

- **dynamic dispatch** (calling through a vtable),
- **async functions** / `impl Trait` (which create compiler-hidden concrete types),
- and a newer internal solver path already used on stable for some “impossible predicate” checks.

Async functions are relevant because they don’t return a simple named type; they return a hidden compiler-generated state machine. Internally, the compiler treats those as **opaque types**: “I know there is a concrete type here, but I’m hiding its exact identity.” In 1.98, after a change related to how the solver handled these hidden types, one code path in later compilation stages (“post-analysis” / code generation) failed to re-check the question in the original typing context when opaques were involved.

That sounds abstract, but the consequence was concrete: the compiler saw an opaque async-related type in the wrong mode, treated it too rigidly, decided some required conditions were impossible, and concluded “this trait method can never be called.” So when it generated the vtable, it emitted a **vacant slot** instead of a real function pointer.

The fix was small but important: when the solver hits this specific “opaque type involved” situation during later compilation stages, it must **rerun the evaluation in the original typing mode**, where the hidden type can be handled correctly. That restored the correct answer (“yes, this method exists”), so the compiler once again generated a valid vtable entry.

This fix had already landed on nightly, which is why nightly builds worked again. It had simply missed Rust 1.98 because the stable release branch was cut between the regression and the later fix. The Rust team backported the missing patch and released **Rust 1.98.1**, which fixed the miscompilation.

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust programs often use “interfaces” so one piece of code can work with many different concrete implementations. When a program chooses one implementation at runtime (instead of at compile time), Rust—like many compiled languages—uses a behind-the-scenes lookup table called a *vtable* (“virtual method table”). You can picture a vtable as a small card catalog attached to an object: it contains pointers to the machine-code functions that implement each interface method for that object. When the program calls an interface method, it looks up the right function pointer in the vtable and jumps to it.

In Rust 1.98, the compiler sometimes generated a vtable where one of the method entries was accidentally left empty—literally a zero pointer. The affected program used a common pattern: storing an “async service” (a component that will do work later, often used in networking) inside a box so it can be called through an interface without knowing its exact type. The program was entirely “safe Rust” (no manual memory tricks), so it should never be able to jump to address zero. But because the compiler produced a vtable with a vacant slot, the program’s perfectly normal interface call looked up the method, got “0”, and attempted to execute code at memory address 0. Operating systems treat that as an invalid access, so the process crashed with a segmentation fault (SIGSEGV). Depending on optimization and circumstances, it could also produce other kinds of “undefined behavior” effects, such as illegal-instruction crashes or incorrect program behavior rather than an immediate crash.

Why would a compiler leave a blank entry in a vtable? Rust’s compiler tries to prove which interface implementations are possible. If it concludes “this method can never be called for this type,” it may treat that slot as unreachable. In 1.98, a change related to Rust’s newer trait solver (the subsystem that reasons about which implementations apply) sometimes made the compiler incorrectly decide that certain requirements (“predicates”) were impossible during the late compilation phases that generate machine code (“post-analysis/codegen”). In this case, complex type relationships involving hidden types created by `async` functions (and similar “opaque” types) triggered the mistake. The compiler’s reasoning went down a path where it treated a required method as unattainable, and the vtable entry ended up marked “vacant,” becoming a null pointer in the final binary.

The issue did not reproduce in earlier Rust (1.97.1) and was already gone in later nightlies/beta, which helped narrow it to a specific regression window. The fix was to ensure that when the compiler encounters these “opaque/hidden” types in the post-analysis/codegen mode, it re-runs the relevant trait reasoning in the original typing mode where those opaque types are handled correctly. Concretely, a decision point in the trait solver that previously said “do not rerun” for a combined condition involving opaques was changed to “yes, rerun.” That prevented the solver from concluding “impossible,” so the compiler kept the real method in the vtable instead of inserting a null.

Because this was a severe miscompilation affecting safe code, the fix was backported as a patch release: Rust 1.98.1.

</div>

Rust’s compiler produced a bad executable from valid, safe Rust code. The program itself was not doing anything wrong.

The crash involved dynamic dispatch, which is how Rust calls methods when the exact concrete type is hidden behind a trait object, like “some service that can handle this request.” To make such calls fast, the compiled program carries a small table of function addresses next to the hidden value. This table is called a vtable. You can think of it as a contact list: “to call `serve`, jump to this address.”

In Rust 1.98.0, the compiler sometimes generated a vtable where one of those contact-list entries was empty: it contained zero instead of the address of the method. Later, ordinary safe Rust code tried to call the method through that entry. The CPU jumped to address zero, which is not valid program code, so the process crashed with a segmentation fault.

The important part is why the compiler put zero there. Rust sometimes omits a method from a vtable if it proves that the method can never legally be called for that particular type. That is normally sound: if a method’s required conditions are impossible, no real call should ever reach it. In this case, however, the proof was wrong. The compiler incorrectly decided that a method’s type requirements could not be satisfied, even though they could.

The deeper cause was in Rust’s trait-solving machinery: the part of the compiler that answers questions like “does this type implement this trait?” and “is this associated output type equal to that one?” Rust has an older and a newer trait solver. The newer solver is not fully the default everywhere, but it is already used internally for some compiler decisions, including checks for “impossible predicates” when building vtables.

A recent compiler change added stricter handling for “opaque” types — types whose exact identity is hidden, such as the return type of an `async fn` or `impl Trait`. These are like sealed boxes: outside code knows what promises the type makes, but not its exact shape. The compiler sometimes analyzes code in a mode where those boxes remain sealed, and later in a mode where their contents are known.

The bug was that, in one combined situation involving opaque types and inference placeholders, the compiler should have re-run a trait check in the more precise mode, but did not. It kept the earlier, less-informed answer. That answer made a valid method look impossible, so the vtable generator emitted a vacant slot.

That also explains why the reproducer looked strange: `async` functions, boxed services, trait objects, and layered generic types created just the right chain of hidden return types and trait obligations to trigger the bad path. Smaller reproducers showed that even a function returning `impl Sized` could trigger it, because the key ingredient was the opaque return type, not networking or Rama itself.

The fix was small but important: when this combined opaque-type condition appears during later compiler phases, Rust now re-runs the trait check in the original, more appropriate typing mode. That change had already landed on nightly, which is why nightly builds no longer reproduced the crash. It was then backported to stable and released as Rust 1.98.1, along with regression tests ensuring that the compiler no longer emits a null vtable entry for this case.

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

In August 2026, a developer named Glen noticed something alarming: after upgrading to Rust version 1.98, his networking software began crashing consistently. The program would hit a specific piece of code and immediately die with a "segmentation fault" — the classic sign that a program has tried to access memory it shouldn't. Crucially, the same code had worked perfectly in the previous version (1.97.1), and it also worked in newer experimental builds. Something introduced in 1.98 was causing crashes.

This was a big deal because Rust is famous for its safety guarantees. The language is designed to make it very hard to write code that crashes in this particular way. Glen mentioned that in eleven years of using Rust in production, this was the first time he'd hit a bug in the stable compiler itself. When the compiler misbehaves, even correctly written code can break.

To understand what went wrong, imagine a restaurant with a phone directory of chefs. When you call in an order, the maître d' looks up which chef handles that dish and rings them. Now imagine the phone directory was printed with one entry accidentally left blank. When an order comes in for that dish, the maître d' dials "nothing" — and disaster ensues.

In programming terms, this "directory" is called a *vtable* (virtual table). When code uses generic interfaces without knowing exactly which specific implementation will be plugged in, the compiler builds these lookup tables so the right function can be called at runtime. In Glen's case, the Rust 1.98 compiler was generating a vtable with a critical entry that was just zero — a null pointer. When the running program tried to make a call through that entry, it jumped to memory address zero, which is invalid, and the operating system killed the program.

Why did this happen? Rust has been undergoing a long-term overhaul of its "trait solver" — the part of the compiler that figures out which pieces of code satisfy which requirements. Think of it as a logic engine that verifies statements like "yes, this type does support that operation." A change made in May 2026 introduced an optimization: the solver would sometimes mark certain type relationships as fixed and skip re-checking them. Unfortunately, in specific edge cases, the solver was concluding that certain requirements were "impossible to satisfy" when they actually were satisfiable in the current context. When the compiler thinks a function can never be called, it doesn't bother emitting a real pointer to it — hence the zero in the vtable.

The bug was particularly nasty because it involved a very specific combination of features: boxed asynchronous services, dynamic dispatch, and complex nested type constraints. Most code that used similar patterns worked fine; only certain configurations triggered the misbehavior.

The fix, actually made in July 2026, addressed a related situation: when the compiler transitions between different "modes" of analysis (roughly, different stages of understanding a program), it needs to re-verify certain conclusions. A missing case in this logic meant some verifications were being skipped incorrectly. Once the Rust team realized this same fix would also solve Glen's crash, they backported it to a patch release, 1.98.1, released on September 3, 2026.

Glen also added a new practice to his workflow: running his full test suite against Rust's pre-release "beta" builds daily. That way, if a similar bug ever slips in again, he might catch it before it reaches a stable release affecting production code.

Later, perfectly safe Rust code tried to call that method through the vtable. Calling address zero is like trying to jump to nowhere, so the program crashed, usually with a segmentation fault.

Rust’s compiler does a lot of logical reasoning about types and conditions. Even though the full “next solver” is not a stable user-facing feature, part of that machinery was already being used on stable for deciding whether certain trait requirements were impossible. A change merged before 1.98 added a concept called “rigid aliases,” used to avoid repeatedly re-normalizing some type expressions. That was fine in principle, but in one path the compiler failed to re-run its reasoning in the original typing mode when opaque or hidden types were involved. Because of that, it compared types in the wrong form, concluded the needed implementation could not exist, and marked the method slot as absent.

That is why the bug appeared in 1.98.0, did not appear in 1.97.1, and had already disappeared in later nightly builds: a separate later fix had already corrected the rerun behavior, but it missed the beta cutoff for 1.98.0.

The fix for stable was very small but important: when the solver encounters certain opaque-type conditions during later compilation stages, it must rerun the check in the original typing mode instead of trusting the first result. Once that rerun happens, the compiler correctly sees that the method does exist, includes it in code generation, and writes a valid function pointer into the vtable.

Rust programs sometimes use “dynamic dispatch”: instead of calling a specific function directly, they store a value together with a little table of function pointers describing how to operate on that value. You can think of it like a universal remote: the remote has buttons (“call this method”, “drop it”, “clone it”), and inside it is a table telling it what exact machine-code address to jump to for each button, depending on what brand of device it’s controlling. In Rust, that table is called a *vtable*.

In Rust 1.98, the compiler occasionally produced a vtable where one of the entries—the one corresponding to a method that should be callable—was incorrectly left empty (it contained the number 0, meaning “no address”). The program itself was written in safe Rust, so it reasonably assumed the “remote” was valid. When it pressed the button, the CPU tried to jump to address 0, which is not executable memory, and the operating system killed the process (on macOS this shows up as a SIGSEGV at pc=0). In the real application (a proxy server), this manifested as a sudden crash during a request instead of returning an error response like “502 Bad Gateway”.

Why would the compiler ever leave a vtable slot empty? Rust can omit entries when it proves a method can never actually be called—similar to a tool that removes “dead code” to optimize. That proof relies on the compiler’s internal logic about types and constraints (rules like “this method exists only if type X satisfies condition Y”). In this case the logic was wrong: the compiler decided the method was “impossible” to call, even though it was reachable at runtime.

The root cause was a subtle interaction with Rust’s “next” trait solver (the part that reasons about those conditions), and with “opaque” types (types whose exact implementation is deliberately hidden, such as the anonymous types created by `async fn` and `impl Trait`). During late compilation stages (post-analysis/code generation), the solver tried to evaluate whether certain trait requirements held. It encountered hidden/opaque types and made a conservative note: “this result might be different if we re-check it in the original typing mode.” Rust already had a mechanism to “rerun” that reasoning under the right mode, but in one combined case—when two different “rerun” triggers were present at once—it mistakenly decided *not* to rerun. That led it to conclude some requirements were unsatisfiable, which then caused the compiler to emit a vtable with a “vacant” (null) entry rather than a proper function pointer.

The fix was to correct that rerun decision: if the solver hits this combined opaque-type condition during post-analysis/codegen, it must rerun the evaluation in the original typing mode. That change had already landed on newer nightly/beta compilers (explaining why the bug disappeared there), but it missed Rust 1.98.0 due to timing. The Rust team backported the fix into a patch release, Rust 1.98.1, and added a regression test to ensure vtables are no longer generated with null method pointers in this scenario.

</div>

<h2 id="codex--gpt-5.6-sol">20. gpt-5.6-sol · ChatGPT sub</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust programs are normally protected from calling invalid memory by the language’s safety rules. In this case, however, a bug in the Rust compiler itself produced an invalid executable from valid, safe Rust code.

The affected project, Rama, uses “boxed services”: objects whose exact concrete type is hidden so that different services can be handled through one common interface. Internally, Rust implements this kind of flexibility with a **virtual method table**, or vtable. A vtable is like a directory attached to an object. It contains basic information about the object and the addresses of the functions that implement its operations. When the program asks a boxed service to handle a request, it looks up the appropriate address in this table and jumps there.

Rust 1.98 generated a table whose basic entries—such as the object’s size and alignment—were correct, but whose first method entry contained zero instead of a function address. When Rama attempted to call that method, the processor jumped to address zero. On the reporter’s Mac this caused an immediate segmentation fault. With optimization enabled, the consequences could vary because the optimizer assumes that valid Rust programs never make such calls; examples included illegal-instruction crashes and even incorrect behavior without an immediate crash. In other words, this was genuine undefined behavior introduced by the compiler, not by the application.

The underlying cause was in Rust’s new system for reasoning about type constraints. Before generating a vtable entry, the compiler checks whether the method’s requirements can possibly be satisfied. If it concludes that they cannot, it may deliberately leave the slot vacant because it believes no valid program could ever call it.

Rust 1.98 sometimes reached that conclusion incorrectly when several advanced type-system features interacted: associated types, hidden “opaque” return types—often produced by asynchronous functions or other `impl Trait` return values—and checks performed during the later code-generation stage. The compiler had previously introduced a notion of “rigid” type aliases to avoid repeatedly resolving types. Such an alias is only valid under the particular assumptions in which it was created. During later analysis, Rust occasionally reused a result from a different reasoning mode instead of reevaluating it under the original assumptions. That made a valid method appear impossible. The compiler consequently omitted the concrete method and emitted a zero vtable slot.

This explains why the bug appeared only with a particular, complicated combination of types, despite Rama using boxed services elsewhere. It also explains why changing apparently unrelated details—or replacing the dynamic service interface with a stored function pointer—avoided the failure.

The regression was introduced shortly before Rust 1.98 by the rigid-alias work. A broader correction had already landed on the development branch before the issue was reported, which is why newer nightly and beta compilers worked. Unfortunately, the release cutoff placed the regression in Rust 1.98 without placing that correction there too.

The fix made the compiler rerun the relevant type check in the proper typing mode whenever opaque types are encountered during late analysis or code generation. This prevents valid predicates from being classified as impossible and ensures that real method addresses are placed in vtables. The correction was backported, accompanied by a small regression test reproducing the dangerous pattern, and released in Rust 1.98.1.

</div>

<h2 id="codex--gpt-5.5">21. gpt-5.5 · ChatGPT sub</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

This was a Rust compiler bug that made some perfectly valid programs crash, even though the programs were written in safe Rust. That is serious because one of Rust’s main promises is that safe Rust should not be able to cause low-level memory errors such as jumping to address zero.

The affected program used a common Rust pattern: storing a service behind a “boxed” interface. Imagine you have many different kinds of machines, but you put them all behind the same front panel with one button labeled “serve.” The rest of the program does not need to know which machine is behind the panel; it just presses the button.

At the machine-code level, Rust implements this kind of “front panel” using a small table of function pointers called a vtable. The table says, in effect: “to destroy this object, call this function; to ask its size, use this value; to call its `serve` method, jump to this address.” When the program invokes the boxed service, it looks up the right function address in the vtable and jumps there.

In Rust 1.98.0, the compiler sometimes produced a vtable where the method slot was empty: it contained zero instead of the address of the real method. So when the program tried to call the service, it loaded the zero address and jumped to it. On macOS this showed up as a segmentation fault with the program counter exactly at zero.

The original report came from the Rama project, a Rust networking framework. Their proxy example worked on Rust 1.97.1, crashed on Rust 1.98.0, and worked again on a later nightly compiler. That strongly suggested a compiler regression rather than an application bug.

The deeper cause involved Rust’s trait solver, the part of the compiler that reasons about whether a type satisfies some set of requirements. In this case, the compiler was trying to decide whether a particular method should exist in the vtable. Because of a subtle interaction involving opaque types — types whose exact identity is hidden, such as those created by `async fn` or `impl Trait` — the compiler incorrectly concluded that the method’s requirements were impossible to satisfy.

Once it believed the method was impossible, it treated the vtable slot as “vacant.” But the program could in fact reach that method through normal safe Rust code. So the compiler’s reasoning and the generated executable disagreed, producing undefined behavior.

The regression was traced to Rust pull request #156742, which changed how the newer trait solver handles certain “rigid” aliases. A later pull request, #158993, had already fixed the underlying issue on nightly and beta by making the solver retry certain checks in the correct typing mode when opaque types are involved. However, Rust 1.98.0 was released in the gap between the regression and that fix.

The fix was therefore to backport #158993 to the stable 1.98 branch. That happened in pull request #161555, which became Rust 1.98.1. The patch changed the solver so that when it encounters this particular combination of hidden/opaque type information during later compiler phases, it reruns the check in the original mode instead of accepting the wrong “impossible” result.

Rust 1.98.1 also added regression tests, including a small minimized example, to ensure the compiler no longer emits a vtable with a missing method slot in this situation.

In short: Rust 1.98.0’s compiler sometimes convinced itself that a real method could never be called, left its vtable entry blank, and generated a crashing binary. Rust 1.98.1 fixed the compiler’s reasoning so the method is recognized and emitted correctly.

</div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

# A Compiler Bug That Made Safe Programs Crash

Most programmers trust their tools completely. When you write code in a language like Rust—famous for its safety guarantees—you expect that if your program compiles, it won't suddenly crash for mysterious reasons. This is a story about a rare case where that trust was betrayed by a bug not in anyone's program, but in the Rust *compiler* itself.

## What Went Wrong

The person who reported this bug, GlenDC, maintains a piece of networking software. It had been working fine for years. Then, when he upgraded to Rust version 1.98, his program started crashing with a "segmentation fault"—a serious error that means the program tried to access a memory address it shouldn't, in this case address zero. Notably, the exact same source code worked perfectly with the previous version (1.97) and with a later experimental version. Only 1.98 was broken.

To understand the crash, imagine a phone book of function "addresses" that the program uses to look up which piece of code to run at a given moment. Programmers call this a "vtable." When the program needed to call a certain function, it looked up the entry in this phone book—and found the number *zero* written there instead of a real address. It dutifully "dialed" zero, and the program collapsed.

## Why It Happened

The root cause lies deep in how the compiler reasons about code. When Rust compiles a program, it does a lot of logical deduction to figure out which functions are actually needed and reachable. Part of this involves checking whether certain conditions ("predicates") are even *possible*. If the compiler concludes that a piece of code could never possibly be reached—because its requirements are logically impossible to satisfy—it optimizes it away and leaves a blank (a zero) in its place.

The bug was that the compiler *incorrectly* decided that a certain function was impossible to reach when in fact it was perfectly valid. So it left a zero where a real function should have been. Then, at runtime, the program actually *did* reach that spot, followed the zero, and crashed.

The deeper technical culprit involves Rust's "trait solver"—the part of the compiler that performs this logical reasoning. Rust has an older solver and a newer, experimental one. Even though most of the language still uses the old solver, certain narrow checks (including the "is this even possible?" check) had quietly begun relying on the new solver. A change made in one update (PR #156742) introduced a subtle flaw in the new solver's handling of a specialized concept, and because a version cutoff happened at an unlucky moment, this flaw shipped in stable Rust 1.98 before the eventual fix had been merged.

Interestingly, the bug only surfaced in one very specific place in GlenDC's large codebase, even though he used the same programming patterns elsewhere—a reminder of how these bugs depend on precise, rare combinations of conditions.

## How It Was Fixed

A separate fix (PR #158993) had already corrected the underlying flaw in the newer solver, originally to solve an unrelated problem. Once the Rust team identified this crash as the same root cause, they "backported" that fix into a small patch release, Rust 1.98.1, which was shipped roughly two weeks after the report.

Because this was a genuine miscompilation—rare enough that GlenDC hadn't hit one in eleven years of professional Rust use—the team treated it seriously, since it silently produced broken programs from correct code.

</div>

Accessibility is strong—jargon is introduced with plain-language glosses, headings aid navigation, and PR numbers are kept to light references.</p></details>
