<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

# What Went Wrong

Modern software compilers don't just translate code line-by-line — they perform deep analysis to figure out what's possible and what isn't, then use that analysis to generate faster, leaner machine code. The Rust compiler (rustc) has a component called a "trait solver" that works out, at compile time, which combinations of types and behaviors are logically valid. This matters enormously for a feature called "dynamic dispatch," where a program doesn't know exactly which specific function it needs to call until the program is actually running — think of it like a restaurant that has a generic "waiter" role, and only at serving time do you find out whether it's Alice or Bob who shows up. To make this work efficiently, the compiler builds a lookup table (a "vtable") containing pointers to the correct functions for each type, and the running program consults that table to figure out where to jump.

Here's where things went wrong: in June 2026, a change was made to the trait solver's logic (in pull request #156742) that altered how it decides whether certain type combinations are even *possible*. In one narrow, unusual scenario — involving asynchronous functions used together with generic type constraints, exactly the pattern found in the "rama" networking library that surfaced this bug — the solver incorrectly concluded that a particular function's requirements could *never* be satisfied. Believing this code path to be genuinely impossible, the compiler decided there was no need to generate machine code for it, and left a blank, zeroed-out entry in the vtable instead of a real function pointer.

The problem is that the code path *was* actually reachable at runtime. When the program ran and tried to look up that function in the table, it found a zero instead of a real address, and jumped straight to memory address zero — which doesn't correspond to any valid code. This caused an immediate crash (a segmentation fault), and in some cases with compiler optimizations enabled, subtler and even stranger malfunctions, since "jumping to a forbidden empty pointer" is undefined behavior — the compiler is allowed to assume it will never happen, and can make aggressive optimizations based on that false assumption, sometimes producing bizarre results rather than a simple crash.

This bug had shipped in a stable release (Rust 1.98.0), affecting real production software, but it took considerable detective work to track down. Several outside contributors (not the original bug reporter) devoted significant effort to progressively shrinking the reproduction case from a large real-world networking codebase down to about 30 lines, and then used an automated tool to pinpoint exactly which code change had introduced the regression.

The underlying issue turned out to be that a related fix (from PR #158993, made afterward) had already solved this exact class of problem — but by unlucky timing, that fix landed just *after* the cutoff point for inclusion in the 1.98 release, so 1.98 shipped with the bug still present. Once identified, the Rust team backported that existing fix into an emergency patch release, 1.98.1, restoring the correct (conservative) behavior where the compiler no longer wrongly assumes such code is impossible, ensuring the necessary function pointer actually gets generated.

</div>

Accuracy is generally good with a few small overstatements (line count, async specificity, characterization of #158993). Accessibility is strong; PR numbers appear but don't overwhelm. Calibration is honest about impact and doesn't overclaim, though it presents the mechanism a bit more confidently than the thread's inferential tone.</p></details>

<h2 id="openai--chat-latest">24. chat-latest · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust 1.98.0 contained a rare but serious compiler bug: a completely safe Rust program could be translated into an invalid executable. When that executable tried to call a particular method, it could jump to memory address zero and crash. Because the compiler was producing incorrect machine code, rather than rejecting an incorrect program, this was classified as a miscompilation and prompted the Rust team to issue the 1.98.1 patch release.

The problem appeared in Rama, a Rust networking framework. Rama uses “dynamic dispatch,” a technique that lets a program work with objects whose precise concrete type is no longer known at that point in the code. To make this possible, the compiler creates a hidden table for each suitable type, called a vtable. Roughly speaking, a vtable contains information about the object plus addresses of the functions that should be called for its methods. When the program invokes a method dynamically, it looks up the appropriate address in this table and jumps there.

Rust 1.98.0 generated one such table with an empty method entry. The bookkeeping fields describing the object were present, but the slot that should have held the address of the service method contained zero. Later, perfectly ordinary safe Rust code read that slot and tried to call the function at the recorded address. On the original system that meant jumping to address zero, causing a segmentation fault.

The underlying mistake was not really in vtable construction itself. Earlier compiler work had changed how Rust's newer trait-solving machinery reasons about “opaque” types—types whose exact identity is deliberately hidden behind constructs such as “some type implementing this interface.” That machinery sometimes performs its reasoning in different modes depending on whether Rust is checking source code or preparing it for machine-code generation.

A change merged before Rust 1.98 introduced the idea that certain partially resolved type expressions could be treated as fixed, or “rigid,” within the environment where they had been analyzed. But that assumption was not always valid when the compiler moved into a different analysis mode. In one particular path, involving opaque types, the solver discovered information that should have made it repeat its calculation in the original, appropriate mode. It failed to do so.

That incorrect conclusion then reached a compiler check called `impossible_predicates`. This check determines whether the requirements for a method can ever be satisfied. If they genuinely cannot, there is no callable method to put into the vtable, so an intentionally vacant slot can be appropriate. Here, however, the solver incorrectly decided that the perfectly valid method was impossible. Consequently, the compiler omitted the concrete method during code generation and produced the empty vtable entry.

This explains why an obscure bug in the experimental-looking “next” trait solver affected stable Rust: parts of that solver were already being used internally on stable specifically for checks such as `impossible_predicates`.

The full fix had actually landed upstream shortly after the faulty change, which is why later nightly and Rust 1.99 beta compilers worked. Unfortunately, the Rust 1.98 release branch was cut between the introduction of the bug and its fix. Once the Rama report was minimized and the regression identified, the team backported that fix. It makes the solver rerun the relevant evaluation in the proper typing mode when it encounters these opaque-type conditions, preventing valid methods from being classified as impossible.

Rust 1.98.1, released September 3, 2026, contains that backport and a regression test modeled on the minimized example. Users of 1.98.0 therefore did not need to rewrite Rama's dynamic-dispatch design: the source code was valid. Updating to 1.98.1 fixed the compiler error that had turned it into an invalid executable.

</div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Think of a program as a machine with parts that call each other through a switchboard. For certain calls, the program doesn’t know exactly which part it will need until it’s running, so it keeps a little phone book (a “vtable”) that lists the numbers to dial for each possible call.

In Rust 1.98.0, the compiler sometimes produced a phone book with a blank entry—literally a zero—where a valid number should have been. When the program later tried to make that call, it dialed 0, which on a computer means “jump to nowhere,” causing an immediate crash. In technical terms: the compiler emitted a vtable with a vacant method slot for a boxed, asynchronously callable service; safe Rust code then dispatched through that empty slot and crashed (or, with certain optimizations, behaved unpredictably).

Why did this happen? Rust’s compiler includes a “solver” that reasons about types and capabilities to decide which pieces of code are actually needed. A recent change (part of its newer “next-solver”) improved how it handles “aliases” and “opaque types”—think of these as placeholders the compiler uses while working out details. Under a specific, fairly intricate combination of async code and dynamic dispatch, the solver convinced itself that a particular method could never be called. Believing the method was impossible, the compiler left its entry out of the phone book and put a zero there instead.

The catch is that this conclusion depended on tentative information. In situations involving those placeholders, the compiler is supposed to circle back and re-check its assumptions in a stricter, later phase (akin to a second opinion once more facts are available). A gap in the logic meant this re-check didn’t happen for one combined condition, so the mistaken “this is impossible” verdict stood—and the method slot stayed empty.

This bug first appeared in stable Rust 1.98.0 and showed up reliably in one real project using advanced async patterns. It did not affect Rust 1.97.1, and it was already fixed on nightly and beta by the time the issue was reported, which is why some users saw it gone there: a separate change had taught the solver to rerun its analysis in the original, more cautious mode whenever it encountered these opaque placeholders in that combined scenario.

The fix was straightforward conceptually: ensure the solver always performs that second look when those placeholders are involved, even under the combined condition. Once it does, it no longer prematurely decides the method is impossible, and the compiler fills in the correct phone number in the vtable. The Rust team backported this fix and released Rust 1.98.1, which resolves the miscompilation.

Two final notes. First, while Rust’s safety guarantees protect against many classes of errors, a compiler miscompilation can still produce crashes or odd behavior—this is why such bugs are treated with high urgency. Second, the problem was rare and required a very specific code pattern, which is why it slipped through initially; adding more real-world projects to beta testing, as the reporter volunteered to do, helps catch such edge cases earlier.

</div>

<h2 id="anthropic--claude-opus-5">26. claude-opus-5 · Anthropic API</h2>

<p><strong>78</strong>/100: understanding 32/40 · accuracy 22/30 · accessibility 17/20 · calibration 7/10<br>612 words · 1,183 output tokens (74 reported as reasoning) · effort: adaptive, high</p>

<div class="explainer-flag"><p><strong>Model errors about a real person</strong>: claims the thread does not support.</p><ul><li>“when he upgraded to version 1.98 of the Rust compiler, his program crashed instantly”, “the bug was not in his program”: the thread never gives the reporter's gender.</li></ul></div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

**What happened**

A Rust programmer running a network proxy server found that, when he upgraded to version 1.98 of the Rust compiler, his program crashed instantly — the operating system reported a "segmentation fault," meaning the program tried to run instructions located at memory address zero, which contains nothing at all. The same source code worked perfectly when compiled with the previous version, 1.97.1, and also with a later experimental build. So the bug was not in his program; it was in the compiler itself — a "miscompilation," which is about the worst class of bug a compiler can have, because the programmer's code is correct and the machine code produced from it is not.

**The mechanism: an empty slot in a lookup table**

Rust, like many languages, lets you hide the exact type of a value behind an interface — you can say "this is *some* kind of service that answers requests" without saying which kind. At runtime this is implemented with a *vtable*: a small table of function addresses attached to the value, so that "call the service" becomes "look up entry number three in this table and jump to whatever address is there."

The compiler built the vtable for this program with a hole in it. The entry where the service's function address should have been contained zero. The program dutifully looked up entry number three, found zero, and jumped to address zero. Hence the crash at exactly that address.

**Why the compiler left the hole**

The compiler deliberately leaves such holes in one specific situation. Sometimes a function only exists under conditions that can never actually be satisfied — the requirements attached to it are logically contradictory. Such a function can never legitimately be called, so rather than generating machine code for it, the compiler marks its vtable slot "vacant." This is a legitimate optimization; the check is called *impossible predicates*.

The trouble is that the check answered "yes, impossible" when the truth was "no, perfectly possible." The reason lies in a large ongoing project inside the compiler: replacing the old logic engine that reasons about types (the "trait solver") with a new one. The new solver isn't the default yet, but the impossible-predicates check already uses it — so a bug in the new solver could leak into ordinary, stable Rust.

The specific defect involved *opaque types*, the compiler's name for values whose concrete type is deliberately hidden behind a description like "something that produces text." (Notably, the crash only appeared when an `async` function or a closure — both of which produce opaque types — was involved.) The solver examines types in different "modes": during ordinary type-checking it must respect the hiding, but when generating machine code it is allowed to see through to the real type. A change merged in June 2026 added machinery to re-run an inconclusive deduction in the more revealing mode. But one of the several conditions that should trigger a re-run had been wired up to *not* re-run. So the solver gave up early, concluded the requirements were unsatisfiable, and the vtable slot was blanked out.

**The fix and the aftermath**

A one-word change — flipping that "No" to "Yes" so the deduction is retried in the revealing mode — had already been merged into the development branch in July, which is why newer builds were fine. Unfortunately the faulty change had slipped past the release cut-off while the fix had not, so version 1.98 shipped with the bug. Once diagnosed, the Rust team backported the fix and published version 1.98.1 on 3 September 2026, along with a test case distilled from the original report to ensure the problem never returns.

</div>

The re-run conditions existed already; the fix PR simply changed one condition's decision from No to Yes.</li><li>"the crash only appeared when an <code>async</code> function or a closure — both of which produce opaque types — was involved" — the final regression test uses a plain function returning <code>impl Sized {}</code> with no closure; what is load-bearing is the hidden (opaque) return type, not async or closures as such. <h2 id="codex--gpt-5.6-terra">27. gpt-5.6-terra · ChatGPT sub</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust programs often use *traits*: contracts that say “anything of this kind can perform this operation.” Sometimes the compiler knows the exact concrete kind of thing it is dealing with; other times it only knows that it satisfies a trait. In the latter case, Rust uses a small runtime lookup table called a **vtable** (virtual-method table).

A vtable is effectively a list of function addresses. When a program wants to call a trait method on an unknown concrete value, it looks up the appropriate address in that table and jumps to it.

In this bug, Rust 1.98 occasionally generated a vtable whose first method address was **zero**—an address that does not contain executable code. Safe Rust code then performed a normal trait-method call, followed the zero address, and crashed. On macOS this appeared as a segmentation fault with the program counter equal to zero. Depending on optimization and platform details, such a bad call could also produce other strange results, because jumping through an invalid function pointer is undefined behavior at the machine-code level.

The affected application, Rama, used a common pattern for asynchronous services. It wrapped different kinds of request handlers in a box so they could be stored and called through one uniform interface. This was not inherently incorrect: the same source worked in Rust 1.97.1, worked again in later nightly builds, and was fixed in Rust 1.98.1.

The underlying problem was in the compiler’s reasoning about types, rather than in the application’s networking code or asynchronous logic.

Rust’s compiler has a trait solver: a component that tries to prove facts such as “this type implements this trait” or “this associated type has this required property.” During compilation, the solver may encounter types whose exact identity is intentionally hidden—for example, the unnamed type returned by an `async fn` or by a function returning `impl Trait`. These are often called **opaque types**.

A recent compiler change added a concept called “rigid aliases,” intended to prevent certain type expressions from being normalized or rewritten in the wrong compilation context. That change exposed a subtle bug in the trait solver’s fallback behavior. In some code-generation and post-analysis situations involving opaque types, the solver reached an uncertain or apparently failing conclusion but did not retry the question in the original, more appropriate typing mode.

As a result, the compiler incorrectly concluded that the conditions required for a particular trait implementation were impossible. Rust has an optimization mechanism for such supposedly impossible cases: when constructing a vtable, it may leave an entry vacant because it believes no valid call can ever reach it. Here that belief was wrong. The program could and did reach the entry, but the compiler had emitted a zero in its place.

The issue was especially dangerous because the compiler accepted the program without warning and generated a binary that could crash in ordinary safe code.

The fix was small but important. Rust already knew to rerun certain trait-solving evaluations in the original typing mode when opaque types appeared. However, one combined condition—roughly, “an opaque type was encountered, or an opaque type had an inferred hidden type”—was mistakenly excluded from that retry during post-analysis and code generation. The patch changed that decision from “do not rerun” to “rerun.”

With the retry, the compiler evaluates the type constraints in the correct context, recognizes that the trait implementation is valid, generates the required service method, and places its real address in the vtable. Rust 1.98.1 shipped this backport along with regression tests designed to ensure that valid vtable entries are not again discarded as impossible.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5-pro-2025-10-06">28. gpt-5-pro-2025-10-06 · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Think of a program’s “call me” list as a little phone book. For features that are chosen at runtime (Rust calls them trait objects), the compiler builds a tiny table, a vtable, that lists which number to dial for each method. When your code says “call the method,” the processor looks up the number in that table and jumps there.

In Rust 1.98, the compiler sometimes wrote a vtable with a blank entry for a real method. When the program later tried to call through that blank entry, the processor jumped to address zero — which on modern systems is invalid — and the program crashed (a segfault). On some builds it could also misbehave in other ways (UB: undefined behavior), like illegal-instruction traps or even wrong logic, because the optimizer may assume “calling through a null pointer never happens” and rearrange code accordingly.

Why did the compiler leave that slot blank? It made a wrong “this can never happen” decision while reasoning about types. Recent Rust compilers use a sophisticated “trait solver” — a logic engine that answers questions like “under these conditions, does this type really provide that method?” To speed and structure this reasoning, the solver sometimes looks at types in different “modes,” and it treats some details as temporarily hidden “opaques” (for example, the hidden return type of an async function). A June change (adding a “rigid alias” marker) made the solver hold on to some of these hidden forms more firmly.

Here’s where the bug appeared: during a late compilation phase (post-analysis/code generation), if the solver bumped into certain opaque details while checking whether a method’s preconditions could ever be met, it was supposed to say, “I might be looking with blurry glasses; rerun this check in the original, clearer mode.” In one combined case — signaled internally as “OpaqueInStorageOrAnyOpaqueHasInferAsHidden” — that rerun didn’t happen. The solver then incorrectly concluded, “this method’s conditions are impossible,” and told the vtable builder to mark the entry as vacant. The code still tried to call it, and boom.

This slipped into stable 1.98 because even though the “next” solver is still being rolled out, one stable pass (impossible_predicates) already uses it to decide which vtable slots to fill. So a solver mistake there directly miscompiled binaries.

It was most visible in a real project (Rama) that uses a popular pattern: boxing an async “service” behind a trait object and calling it dynamically. The same code worked on 1.97.1, crashed on 1.98.0, and worked again on a recent nightly and on beta — a strong sign of a compiler regression.

The fix was straightforward once identified: whenever that specific opaque-related condition occurs in the late phase, always rerun the check in the original typing mode. That forces the solver to “take off the blurry glasses,” reveal enough about the opaque types, and avoid the false “impossible” conclusion. This change (PR #158993) was already in nightly/beta and was backported to 1.98.1 (PR #161555), along with tests that recreate the scenario to prevent regressions.

In the meantime, the reporter found workarounds (rewriting code to avoid that erased async-vtable boundary), but with 1.98.1 released, users should simply upgrade. The episode is a reminder that miscompilations can be subtle, and that running real-world test suites on beta helps catch rare but impactful issues early.

</div>

<h2 id="moonshot--kimi-k2.7-code-highspeed">29. kimi-k2.7-code-highspeed · Moonshot API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Imagine a compiler as a meticulous translator that turns human-readable recipes into precise kitchen instructions. One thing it produces is a "menu map" — a table telling the kitchen which station prepares each dish when the exact dish isn't known until dinnertime. In programming, this map is called a *vtable*.

In Rust 1.98, the translator made a dangerous mistake. It produced a menu map with one entry blank, pointing to "Kitchen 0." When the restaurant opened and a customer ordered that dish, the waiter looked at the map, saw "Kitchen 0," and walked into a wall. In computer terms, the program crashed with a segmentation fault — it tried to run code at memory address zero, which doesn't exist.

The root cause lay in an ambitious speed-up the compiler had recently learned. To avoid rechecking the same type identities over and over, the compiler could mark certain type aliases as "rigid," meaning "trust me, we already know what this is." This is fine during early planning, but problems arise when the compiler moves from planning to actual code generation. Some types — especially opaque types hidden behind phrases like "impl Iterator" — contain details that are only filled in later. Under specific conditions, the compiler skipped a second look it should have taken. It wrongly concluded that a particular method could never be reached, so it left the corresponding vtable slot empty.

Why did this affect only some programs? The bug required a delicate combination: dynamic dispatch through a boxed service, complex generic constraints, and an opaque return type whose hidden contents contained unresolved variables. Most programs don't hit all three at once, which is why the issue lurked in one corner of the ecosystem rather than breaking everything.

The fix, developed by the Rust team and released as Rust 1.98.1, tells the compiler to rerun its evaluation in the original planning mode whenever it encounters these hidden inferred types during code generation. In effect, the translator now double-checks the menu before printing the final map. If a method really is unreachable, the slot stays empty only after proper verification; otherwise, it gets filled in correctly.

This case is a good reminder that compilers are among the most complex pieces of software humans build. Even a subtle change meant to make type-checking faster can accidentally produce invalid machine instructions. The Rust project fixed it quickly because an affected user reported a reproducible crash, and the community rapidly reduced it to a tiny example that revealed the faulty logic.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.1">30. gpt-5.1 · OpenAI API</h2>

<p><strong>76</strong>/100: understanding 29/40 · accuracy 25/30 · accessibility 15/20 · calibration 7/10<br>669 words · 876 output tokens · effort: none</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Imagine you are assembling a detailed instruction manual for how to use a particular gadget. At the end, you create an index card: on page 1 you can find “how to turn it on”, on page 2 “how to turn it off”, and so on. Now imagine that, due to a paperwork mistake, the card says: “for ‘turn it on’, go to page 0” – and page 0 doesn’t exist. Anyone following that index card will crash into a dead end.

This bug in Rust 1.98 is the same kind of error, but in compiled machine code.

### What’s the “index card” here?

Rust uses “vtables” (virtual tables) to support dynamic dispatch – roughly, “calling something by its role rather than by its exact type”. A vtable is like an array of function pointers: slot 0 is “how to drop (destroy) this thing”, slot 1 might be “how big is it”, slot 3 “call this object as a service”, etc.

In this case, code was using a “boxed async service”: a generic, reusable “do some work for me asynchronously” object hidden behind an interface. Rustc (the Rust compiler) automatically built a vtable for this service. One of the entries was supposed to be “how to call the service”. Instead, that entry was left as a null pointer – the equivalent of “page 0” in our index card.

At runtime, normal Rust code followed that vtable entry, jumped to address zero, and the operating system quite reasonably killed the program with a segmentation fault (a crash at memory address 0). That’s why the user saw a SIGSEGV with the program counter at 0.

### Why did the compiler leave that slot empty?

Rust’s type system is very rich: it tracks not only what type something is, but also conditions (“predicates”) under which an operation is valid. The compiler has to answer questions like: “Is it always possible to call this method, for every situation where this type might appear?”

Internally, a newer, more advanced “trait solver” (a reasoning engine for these type predicates) is used in some places, including a mechanism called “impossible predicates”. That mechanism helps the compiler conclude “this method can never actually be used” and then safely omit it.

In Rust 1.98, a change to that solver introduced a subtle logic error. Under some very specific, complex conditions involving async functions and generic traits, the solver wrongly concluded that the method was impossible to call, even though it really was possible in practice. As a result, when it generated the vtable, it put a “vacant” entry (a zero) where the method address should be. No error, no warning – just a silently broken vtable.

Because vtables are used by safe Rust code, this turns into undefined behavior at runtime: the compiler has told the CPU to jump to “nowhere”.

### Why only some code, and why only Rust 1.98?

Most Rust code doesn’t create exactly the peculiar combination of async functions, trait constraints, and dynamic dispatch that triggers this bug. The Rama project happened to hit the edge case: a boxed async “service” behind layers of generic wrappers.

Rust 1.97 didn’t yet include this specific solver change, so it didn’t misclassify the method as “impossible”. Later nightlies and Rust 1.99 beta had an additional fix that corrected the solver’s behavior, which is why they also behaved correctly.

### How was it fixed?

The fix is conceptually simple: whenever the trait solver realizes, during a later phase, “oh, I encountered certain kinds of opaque types or hidden details,” it must re-run its reasoning in a more faithful mode, rather than assume “this can never happen.” A pull request (later backported into Rust 1.98.1) changed the solver so that in those cases it re-evaluates the situation, correctly deciding that the method is in fact possible and must be included in the vtable.

With that correction, the compiler once again fills in a real function pointer instead of zero, and the code runs normally instead of jumping into empty memory.

</div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust 1.98 contained a compiler bug that could turn ordinary safe Rust code into a program that crashed or behaved unpredictably. The affected programs used a common pattern: putting different kinds of “services” behind a boxed, dynamically dispatched interface so they could be called through a uniform handle.

A useful analogy is a remote control and a device manual. When Rust stores a value behind a trait object—a dynamically dispatched interface—it keeps two things:

1. the actual value, and  
2. a small table of function addresses describing how to operate on that value.

That table is called a *vtable*. Its first entries describe housekeeping such as how to destroy the value and its size. Later entries are addresses of methods such as “serve this request.” When code calls a method dynamically, it looks up the appropriate address in the vtable and jumps to it.

In Rust 1.98, the compiler sometimes generated a vtable whose housekeeping entries were correct but whose first method entry was zero—effectively a blank address. The program would then attempt to call that address. On affected machines this commonly meant jumping to address zero and immediately crashing with a segmentation fault. In the reported web-proxy application, a failed upstream network connection should have produced a normal “502 Bad Gateway” response. Instead, the proxy process crashed.

This was especially serious because the application used no unsafe code. Rust’s safety guarantees mean safe code should not be able to cause this kind of invalid indirect call. Once the compiler had emitted the bad table, however, the resulting executable had undefined behavior: it might crash, execute an illegal instruction, or—in optimized builds—produce stranger behavior.

The bug was introduced by compiler work intended to improve Rust’s “next-generation trait solver,” the subsystem that reasons about generic type constraints. Rust programs often contain statements like “this type implements this interface, provided that several other conditions hold.” The compiler must prove such conditions when choosing implementations and generating code.

A recent change added a notion of “rigid aliases”: temporary names for types whose meaning must not be freely reinterpreted in the current reasoning context. That change was mostly correct, but there was a subtle missing step. While examining certain complex generic constraints involving opaque types—types whose concrete form is deliberately hidden, such as those produced by `async fn` or `impl Trait`—the solver could reach an inconclusive or incorrect result during a late compilation phase.

In this situation, the compiler should have repeated the reasoning in the original, more appropriate typing mode. A related fix had already been merged for nightly Rust, which is why later nightly versions did not reproduce the crash. But the condition that triggered the retry was combined with another condition in a way that accidentally suppressed that retry during post-analysis and code generation.

Without the retry, the compiler incorrectly concluded that a method’s requirements were impossible to satisfy. It therefore treated the vtable method entry as “vacant” and emitted a zero instead of generating and recording the actual function.

The fix was tiny but important: when that combined “opaque type encountered” condition appears during post-analysis or code generation, the trait solver now reruns in the original typing mode rather than accepting the erroneous result. Rust developers added regression tests, including a minimized example that creates a trait object and calls its method. The fix was backported in Rust 1.98.1, released on September 3, 2026.

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.5">32. gpt-5.5 · OpenAI API</h2>

<p><strong>74</strong>/100: understanding 28/40 · accuracy 25/30 · accessibility 14/20 · calibration 7/10<br>608 words · 943 output tokens (92 reported as reasoning) · effort: medium</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust 1.98 had a compiler bug that could produce a broken program from perfectly safe Rust code. The affected program was a network proxy from the Rama project. With Rust 1.97.1 it behaved normally; with Rust 1.98.0 it crashed; with later nightly compilers it worked again. That pattern strongly suggested a compiler regression rather than a bug in the application.

The crash involved something called a “vtable.” A vtable is like a small directory of function addresses. When code uses dynamic dispatch — roughly, “I have some object that promises it can do this operation, but I do not know its exact concrete type here” — the compiled program uses the vtable to find the right function to call at runtime. In this case, Rama had a boxed asynchronous service: a value hidden behind an interface, where calling it returns an asynchronous operation. The program expected the vtable to contain the address of the service’s `serve` method.

Rust 1.98 sometimes emitted a vtable where that method slot was zero. So when the program tried to call the service, it looked up the function address, found zero, and jumped to address zero. On macOS this appeared as a segmentation fault with the program counter exactly at zero. In ordinary terms: the compiler produced a phone book entry with a blank phone number, and the running program tried to call it anyway.

The especially serious part is that this happened in safe Rust. Rust’s safety guarantees rely on the compiler correctly translating safe code. A null method entry in a vtable is not something normal safe Rust code should be able to create. So this was a compiler miscompilation: the source code was valid, but the generated machine code was wrong.

The root cause was in Rust’s trait reasoning machinery. Traits are Rust’s way of describing capabilities — for example, “this type can act as a service,” or “this type can be streamed.” During compilation, rustc must decide which trait implementations are possible. For trait objects, it also decides which methods belong in the vtable.

Rust 1.98 included a change related to the newer trait solver, specifically around how certain type aliases and hidden “opaque” types are treated. Opaque types are used in features such as `impl Trait` and async functions, where the source code says “some concrete type exists here, but I am not naming it.” The compiler has different “modes” for reasoning about these types at different phases, such as type checking versus later code generation.

The bug was that, in a particular post-analysis/code-generation situation, the compiler failed to rerun a trait query in the correct mode after encountering opaque types. As a result, it could incorrectly conclude that a method’s required conditions were impossible. Rust’s vtable construction has a concept of a “vacant” entry for methods that cannot exist for a given object. Because the compiler reached the wrong conclusion, it put a vacant entry — effectively zero — where a real method pointer was needed.

The issue was minimized from a large real-world proxy into a tiny reproduction. That helped identify that the regression came from pull request #156742, and that the problem had already been fixed on nightly by pull request #158993. The fix was conceptually small: when the solver encounters these opaque-type conditions during post-analysis or code generation, it must rerun the evaluation in the original typing mode instead of accepting the misleading result.

Because Rust 1.98.0 stable could generate crashing binaries from safe code, the fix was backported promptly. Rust 1.98.1 was released with the correction and a regression test to ensure vtables are not generated with missing method entries in this scenario.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.4-pro-2026-03-05">33. gpt-5.4-pro-2026-03-05 · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust 1.98 briefly shipped with a serious compiler bug: it could generate a broken “method lookup table” for certain kinds of code, and then the finished program would crash by trying to call a function at address zero.

A good analogy is a remote control with a table inside saying “button 1 calls function A, button 2 calls function B.” In the buggy compiler, Rust correctly filled in the housekeeping fields of that table, but left one button’s entry blank. Later, perfectly ordinary safe Rust code pressed that button through dynamic dispatch — meaning “call the right implementation based on the real object at runtime” — and the CPU jumped to “nowhere.” On macOS that showed up as a segmentation fault with the program counter equal to zero.

Why did the table end up with a blank slot? The immediate cause was in rustc’s trait-solving logic: the part of the compiler that reasons about whether a type really implements some interface. Rust 1.98 had gained a change around “rigid aliases” and opaque types (compiler-internal ways of representing types whose exact form is temporarily hidden). In one path used during later compilation stages, the solver could analyze a requirement in the wrong “typing mode,” reach the wrong conclusion that a method was effectively impossible or unnecessary, and mark the vtable entry as vacant.

That was especially nasty because another part of the compiler still generated code that assumed the method existed and would be callable through the table. So the compiler became inconsistent with itself: one subsystem erased the method from the table, another emitted a caller that loaded that slot and jumped through it. That mismatch is what caused the crash.

This also explains the version pattern reporters saw:

- Rust 1.97.1: fine
- Rust 1.98.0: broken
- newer nightly builds: fine again

The bug was introduced by one pull request and then effectively fixed later by another, but the stable 1.98 release happened in between those two changes. So nightly already had the fix, while stable did not.

The actual fix was small but important. The compiler already had logic saying “if we run into opaque types during this later analysis, rerun the check in the original typing mode where those hidden types can be handled correctly.” One case had been missed: a combined rerun condition involving opaque types and inferred hidden types. Rust 1.98 said “don’t rerun” there; the fix changed that to “do rerun.” Once the solver reran in the proper mode, it no longer incorrectly concluded that the method was impossible, so the vtable entry was populated correctly.

Because this was a compiler miscompilation affecting safe code, the Rust team treated it as urgent and shipped Rust 1.98.1 specifically to fix it. In short: the program wasn’t doing anything wrong; the compiler built a bad dispatch table, and the patch taught the compiler to double-check that case in the right context before emitting machine code.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5-pro">34. gpt-5-pro · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Think of a “service object” in a program like a little box with two parts: some data and a small phonebook (a table) listing which functions to call when you ask it to “do the thing.” When the program wants the service to act, it looks up the right function in that phonebook and dials the number.

In Rust 1.98, the compiler sometimes produced a broken phonebook for certain advanced, boxed “async” services: the first function slot was filled with 0 instead of a real phone number. At runtime, the program dutifully tried to call whatever was in that slot, effectively dialing number 0, which is “nowhere,” and the operating system stopped the program with a crash. Sometimes it crashed differently (or even behaved oddly without crashing) because once you stray into “undefined behavior,” anything can happen, especially with optimizations.

Why did the compiler do that? The short answer: it wrongly convinced itself that the function in question could never be needed, so it left the entry blank.

The longer version, without jargon: modern Rust uses a sophisticated reasoning engine to decide which pieces of code are applicable in complicated, generic situations—especially with async functions, where the compiler hides a lot of internal detail for you. That engine works in different “modes” depending on what stage of compilation it’s in. A change that landed earlier (a refinement around how the compiler treats certain “opaque” or hidden types) altered how these modes interact. In a narrow but real set of circumstances, the engine reached an “it’s impossible that this function is needed” conclusion while in a mode where some information was still hidden. Crucially, when a later step made that hidden information visible, the engine failed to go back and re-check the conclusion under the new, clearer view. So the compiler proceeded as if the function truly didn’t exist, and emitted a phonebook entry with zero.

That’s why the bug was slippery: most code paths were fine; only a particular blend of async code, generics, and dynamic dispatch triggered the misjudgment. Projects like Rama hit it because they used precisely that blend.

How was it fixed? The repair was small but important: when the compiler detects that hidden, “opaque” details are involved and could affect the decision, it now re-runs the reasoning step in the original, more suitable mode once those details become visible. In practical terms, it double-checks the earlier “this can’t happen” claim under the right lighting. With that second look, the compiler sees the function is indeed possible and fills in the correct phone number in the service’s phonebook.

This fix first appeared on nightly builds and was then backported to a 1.98.1 patch release, because the original 1.98 behavior could reliably crash safe Rust programs and even cause subtler misbehavior. Tests were added to prevent regressions. While this sounds scary, it’s a rare kind of compiler miscompilation, and the swift patch means users can upgrade to 1.98.1 (or newer) and carry on.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.1-2025-11-13">35. gpt-5.1-2025-11-13 · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Imagine you have a very smart librarian who, instead of remembering every book in detail, keeps index cards. Each card tells her three things:

1. How to destroy the book when you’re done (drop).
2. How big it is (size).
3. Where the book’s actions are stored — for example, “if someone asks to read this book, go to shelf X, position Y.”

In programming terms, that index card is like a *vtable*: a table of pointers (addresses) to pieces of code. When Rust code uses “dynamic dispatch” — calling methods on an object whose exact type isn’t known until the program is running — it looks up the right function in this table and jumps there.

In Rust 1.98, a subtle compiler bug caused some of these “pointers to code” in the table to be zero — literally, an empty address. The compiled Rust program then did something equivalent to: “Please jump to address 0 and run whatever is there.” On modern systems, address 0 is invalid, so the operating system immediately kills the program with a crash like “segmentation fault at address 0.”

So what went wrong?

The code in question used a fairly advanced Rust pattern: “async traits with dynamic dispatch.” In plainer language:

- There were traits (interfaces) with async methods (functions that wait on things, like network I/O).
- These were then wrapped in boxes and passed around as “some service that does X,” without caring which exact concrete type it was.
- The compiler has to create vtables for these boxed services so method calls can be made at runtime.

The newer parts of Rust’s type system (the “next trait solver”) try to be clever: before emitting code, they check whether certain trait requirements are *impossible* to satisfy. If a method could *never* be called because its conditions can never hold, the compiler is allowed to treat that method slot in the vtable as “vacant” and put no function pointer there.

In this bug, the solver mis‑reasoned about some very complex generic and async types. It incorrectly decided a perfectly valid method was “impossible,” so it:

- Generated a vtable with correct housekeeping info (drop, size, alignment),
- But put a zero in the first method slot instead of a real function pointer.

The user’s safe Rust code then did a normal dynamic call through this vtable, hit the zero entry, and jumped to address 0. That’s undefined behavior: the language promises this cannot happen when you stay in safe Rust, but a compiler bug broke that promise.

Why did it only show up in Rust 1.98?

A specific change (the “rigid alias marker” work) altered how the new solver tracks and defers the evaluation of certain type relationships. This interacted badly with opaque and async types and the system that decides when to “rerun” type checking in a more precise mode. In some cases the solver concluded, wrongly, that a method’s conditions could never be satisfied and marked that method as effectively non-existent for code generation.

How was it fixed?

A later change (already in nightly and then backported to 1.98.1) tightened the rules for when the solver must *re-evaluate* its conclusions in a more detailed mode, especially when opaque (“hidden”) types are involved. With that fix:

- The compiler reruns the trait reasoning in the right “mode,”
- Correctly sees that the method is in fact possible,
- Emits a proper function pointer in the vtable slot,
- And the call works as expected — no crashes, no silent misbehavior.

Rust 1.98.1 is essentially 1.98.0 plus that one crucial solver fix (and associated tests), which restores the guarantee that safe Rust code won’t crash due to a null vtable entry.

</div>

Imagine you have a directory of customer-service hotlines. Each service (billing, tech support, sales) gets a name and a phone number. When a call comes in, the receptionist looks up the requested service in that directory and dials the number. Now picture a printing error that leaves the “tech support” phone number blank. If the receptionist tries to dial that entry, they end up dialing “nothing,” which immediately fails.

That’s essentially what happened inside Rust 1.98.0. The Rust compiler creates a small “directory” (called a vtable) whenever code asks to call a component through a general interface rather than a specific implementation. One slot in this table is the address of the actual function to run. In this release, under a very particular combination of modern Rust features—calling an asynchronous service through a boxed trait object—the compiler sometimes printed a vtable where the function slot was zero instead of a real address. When the program later tried to call through that slot, it jumped to address zero and crashed (a segmentation fault). In other cases, because this is undefined behavior, it could look like other kinds of failures too.

Why did the compiler do that? The root cause was a subtle logic change in how Rust reasons about types and capabilities behind the scenes. Rust uses a powerful “trait solver” to prove whether a piece of code is valid: for example, “does this object really support the method I want to call?” A recent change introduced the idea of certain type placeholders being “rigid” in specific contexts, and also tweaked when the solver should re-check its assumptions. Combined with code paths that already use the newer solver for special checks, the compiler could convince itself that a method would never actually be needed in the final program. Concluding it was “impossible,” it didn’t generate the method’s code and left a blank in the vtable—like omitting the tech support number because a prior step wrongly decided no one could ever call it.

A key ingredient in this misjudgment was the presence of “opaque” types—the compiler intentionally hides the exact concrete type of some async and “impl Trait” constructs until the right phase. In Rust 1.98.0, if the solver encountered these opaques at a late stage (“post-analysis” or during code generation), it didn’t always rerun its reasoning in the original mode where those types would be revealed enough to make a sound decision. That’s how it reached the false “impossible” conclusion and emitted a vacant slot.

How was it fixed? The compiler was updated so that whenever it hits these opaque types in that late stage, it reruns the check in the original mode. Put simply: if the directory publisher notices it’s looking at a redacted entry, it goes back to the unredacted source before printing. With that rerun, the solver correctly sees that the method is indeed needed, generates it, and fills in the vtable slot with a proper address. This fix landed quickly on the development branch and was backported to a patch release, Rust 1.98.1. Users who hit the crash on 1.98.0 can update to 1.98.1 to resolve it.

In short: a sophisticated but brittle shortcut in the compiler’s reasoning caused it to omit a needed phone number. The patch teaches it to double-check when information is hidden, ensuring the number makes it into the directory and calls go through safely.

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

# A Software Bug That Made Programs Crash by Calling Empty Phone Numbers

Imagine you have a phone directory where each person's entry contains their name and phone number. When you want to reach someone, you look up their name, find their number, and dial it. Now imagine if, due to a printing error, some entries had blank phone numbers. When you tried to call those people, you'd dial nothing—and in the world of computers, "dialing nothing" causes the program to crash.

This is essentially what happened in version 1.98 of the Rust programming language compiler (the tool that converts human-written code into programs computers can run).

## What Went Wrong

Rust has a feature that allows programs to decide *at runtime* which specific piece of code to run. This is called "dynamic dispatch." To make this work, the compiler creates something called a "vtable"—think of it as that phone directory. Each entry in the vtable points to a specific function the program might need to call.

In this bug, the compiler was creating vtables with empty slots—entries that pointed to address zero (essentially a blank phone number). When the running program tried to use one of these entries, it would attempt to jump to memory address zero, which is forbidden territory in modern operating systems. The computer would immediately terminate the program with a "segmentation fault"—the digital equivalent of a disconnected number tone.

## Why It Happened

The bug originated from a change made to improve how Rust handles complex type calculations. When the compiler needs to figure out which functions go into a vtable, it asks itself: "Is it even possible for this code to be called?" If the answer is "no, that's impossible," it marks the slot as "vacant" and doesn't bother generating the function.

The problem was that a new optimization feature was incorrectly concluding that certain perfectly valid code paths were "impossible." This happened specifically when the code involved a combination of async functions (code that can pause and resume), generic types (code that works with multiple data types), and certain trait bounds (rules about what types can do).

The compiler has two modes for analyzing these situations: a newer, more sophisticated solver and the traditional one. A fix for this exact issue had been developed, but there was an unfortunate timing problem. The buggy code was included in version 1.98, but the fix didn't make it in before the release deadline—it landed in the development branch just days too late.

## How It Was Fixed

The fix itself was remarkably small—just changing a single word in the code from `No` to `Yes`. This change told the compiler: "When you encounter these ambiguous situations during the final code generation phase, don't assume things are impossible. Go back and re-check your work using a different analysis mode."

Once this issue was identified and confirmed, the Rust team released version 1.98.1 within about two weeks, which included this one-line fix.

## The Broader Lesson

This bug is a fascinating example of how compiler optimizations can backfire. The compiler was trying to be clever by skipping work it thought was unnecessary, but its reasoning was flawed in specific edge cases. The original bug reporter noted they had used Rust in production for 11 years without hitting a compiler bug this severe—a testament to how rare such issues are, but also a reminder that even the most carefully designed systems can have subtle flaws.

</div>

<h2 id="moonshot--kimi-k2.7-code">38. kimi-k2.7-code · Moonshot API</h2>

<p><strong>72</strong>/100: understanding 27/40 · accuracy 22/30 · accessibility 17/20 · calibration 6/10<br>434 words · 814 output tokens (276 reported as reasoning) · effort: thinking on</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Imagine you order a meal at a restaurant. The menu lists each dish and points to the kitchen station that makes it. Rust’s job is to prepare that menu, called a *vtable*, so the program can look up a function and run it. In Rust 1.98, the compiler sometimes printed a menu entry that pointed to address zero—literally nowhere. When the program tried to follow that pointer, it crashed with a segmentation fault.

This happened only in specific situations, but it was severe: safe, ordinary-looking Rust code could produce a binary that jumped into invalid memory. The bug first appeared in Rust 1.98, worked fine in 1.97.1, and was already fixed in a later nightly version.

To understand why, it helps to know that Rust checks the types in your program in phases. Early on, the compiler treats certain hidden types as *opaque*—like sealed envelopes whose contents it cannot see yet. The `async fn` keyword and `impl Trait` return types create exactly these envelopes. During type checking, the compiler sometimes decides that a method could never possibly be called because its requirements cannot be satisfied. When it makes that decision correctly, it leaves a blank spot in the vtable, which is harmless because the spot is never used.

In Rust 1.98, a change meant to improve how the compiler handles these opaque envelopes caused it to make that “impossible” decision at the wrong time. It was checking the requirements in a mode where the hidden type inside the envelope was still invisible, so it mistakenly concluded that a valid method could never exist. It therefore wrote a zero into the vtable. But later, when the envelope was opened, it became clear the method *was* valid and *would* be called. The program then branched to address zero and crashed.

The fix came in pull request #158993. It told the compiler: if you encounter one of these opaque envelopes while analyzing code for code generation, go back and re-run the type check in the original, more permissive mode. That gives the compiler a second chance to see the real contents of the envelope and realize the method is possible after all. This corrected the false “impossible” verdict and produced a proper vtable entry.

Because the bug affected stable Rust and caused real crashes in production projects, the Rust team backported the fix and released Rust 1.98.1. The issue was closed once that patch was available. In short, the compiler was prematurely deciding that a function could never be reached while it still had a blindfold on; removing the blindfold at the right moment fixed the crash.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-6-luna">39. gpt-6-luna · OpenAI API</h2>

<p><strong>71</strong>/100: understanding 26/40 · accuracy 25/30 · accessibility 13/20 · calibration 7/10<br>502 words · 1,022 output tokens (423 reported as reasoning) · effort: medium</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust’s compiler generated a broken dispatch table for a valid Rust program. The program used a boxed service: a service stored behind an interface so the program could call it without knowing its concrete type. Rust implements this kind of dynamic dispatch with a *vtable*, a table of function addresses. When the program called the service, it looked up the relevant method in that table. In Rust 1.98, the compiler put zero in the method’s slot instead of the address of the method. Calling address zero caused the reported crash. Depending on how the resulting code was optimized, the mistake could also cause other unpredictable behavior.

The surprising part is that the program was written in safe Rust. It was not supposed to be the programmer’s responsibility to check that the compiler had filled in every vtable entry correctly. The service’s method really did exist; the compiler simply failed to recognize that it applied to this particular service type.

To decide which methods to put in the table, the compiler has to check the rules attached to implementations. Those rules can involve complicated relationships between types. In this case, the program used opaque types—types whose exact identity is hidden from the surrounding code. Async functions are a familiar source of such types: an async function produces a compiler-generated future, but callers do not need to know its precise type. The reproduction also showed the problem with `impl Sized`, so async code was not required; it helped expose the underlying issue.

The failure involved the compiler’s newer trait solver, which reasons about whether types satisfy such rules. During code generation, the solver encountered an opaque type while operating in a special analysis mode. It should have repeated the check in the original typing mode, where the opaque type’s relevant relationships could be assessed correctly. Instead, it treated the implementation’s requirements as impossible. Rustc then left the method slot marked as vacant, but did not report an error. The program compiled, and the bad table was used at runtime.

A compiler change in Rust 1.98 had introduced a more careful way to treat certain type aliases as “rigid”—that is, not to casually expand or reinterpret them in contexts where their meaning might differ. That change exposed the solver’s missing re-check. The issue was subtle enough that the same source worked with Rust 1.97.1 and with a later nightly compiler. The bug also affected stable Rust through a solver path used to determine whether predicates were impossible, even though the newer solver was not used everywhere.

The fix restored the missing re-check: when the solver finds an opaque type in this situation, it now repeats the evaluation in the appropriate typing mode. Rust backported that correction to Rust 1.98.1 and added a regression test based on a minimized version of the bug. The practical lesson is not that boxed services or async Rust are inherently unsafe; rather, this was a compiler error that made valid safe code behave as if a method did not exist.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5-mini">40. gpt-5-mini · OpenAI API</h2>

<p><strong>71</strong>/100: understanding 24/40 · accuracy 25/30 · accessibility 15/20 · calibration 7/10<br>478 words · 952 output tokens (320 reported as reasoning) · effort: medium</p>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

What happened, in plain terms

A recent Rust compiler update (version 1.98) accidentally produced broken machine code in some programs. The broken code contained what looked like a pointer to a function but was instead zero. When the running program tried to call that function it jumped to address zero and crashed the process. That’s what users saw: an otherwise working program suddenly segfaulted when compiled with this particular Rust release.

Why that caused a crash

Many programming languages (including Rust) use a small runtime structure called a vtable to implement “dynamic dispatch” — the ability to call a method on a value whose exact type isn’t known until the program runs. A vtable is simply a table of addresses (pointers) to the correct functions for a concrete object. If the compiler ever emits a vtable entry of zero instead of a real function pointer, the program will try to jump to address zero — which is invalid — and the operating system kills the process. That is what happened here.

What the compiler got wrong

The compiler decides which methods need to be present in a vtable and what code to generate for them by reasoning about types. Rust has some advanced features around generics and “opaque” types (things like the hidden concrete return type of async functions). The compiler’s “solver” is the piece that reasons about those types and predicates.

A change in the compiler’s type-solving logic caused it, in certain obscure situations, to conclude prematurely that a particular method could not exist for the erased/dynamic type. Because of that mistaken conclusion it omitted generating the actual method pointer for the vtable and left a zero there. In other words, the compiler’s reasoning about types was wrong in a narrow case, and that led directly to corrupt vtables and crashes.

How it was fixed

The fix was to make the solver more conservative: when it encounters these opaque or hidden types during a stage of analysis that can lose important information, it reruns the type reasoning in the original context instead of relying on the incorrect, simplified conclusion. That additional re-evaluation prevents the erroneous “impossible method” conclusion and ensures the compiler emits the correct function pointer into the vtable.

Practically, the Rust team identified the regression, wrote a patch to rerun the analysis in the right typing mode, and backported that fix to a point release (1.98.1). Users who hit the crash were advised to upgrade to 1.98.1, which contains the corrected behavior.

Why this was surprising

This was a relatively rare kind of miscompilation: compiler bugs that silently produce wrong code (rather than crashing the compiler itself) are uncommon. Because the problem only showed up in some specific type-and-async combinations, it took careful reduction and bisection to locate the offending change and to craft the conservative correction. The team also added tests to prevent regressions.

</div>

A simplification rather than a clear falsehood.</li><li>"during a stage of analysis that can lose important information" — vague gloss on the actual fix (re-running goal evaluation in the original typing mode when opaque types are encountered in post-analysis/codegen mode); not contradicted by the source but not really what it says either.</li></ul><p>The explanation gives a clear, correct account of the symptom (a zero where a function address should be) and a good plain-language explanation of what a vtable is and why a zero entry crashes the program. <div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Modern computer languages do enormous amounts of bookkeeping for us.  
When you write “ call the service ”, the compiler secretly builds a little
table that says “ to drop the service go to address A, to ask it to work
go to address B ”.  
That table – a “virtual-function table” or *vtable* – is nothing more than
a list of telephone numbers (machine-code addresses) that the program will
dial while it is running.

What happened in Rust 1.98 was that the compiler sometimes wrote **zero**
instead of a real telephone number in the first slot of that list.  
Later, when the program tried to “phone” the service, it obediently read
the first entry, found the number 0, and jumped to address 0.  
On every modern operating system address 0 is unmapped, so the program
instantly crashed with “segmentation fault” – the electronic equivalent of
dialling 000-0000 and getting an electric shock.

Why did the compiler think an empty slot was acceptable?  
Rust needs to *prove* at compile-time that a particular piece of code really
does exist before it puts its address in the vtable.  The proof is carried
out by an intricate logical engine nick-named the “trait solver”.  
Between versions 1.97 and 1.98 a large internal change (#156742) was merged
into this solver. The change introduced the idea of a “rigid alias”: under
certain circumstances the solver was allowed to treat an item of
information as unquestionably fixed and to skip some of the cross-checking
(“renormalisation”) it normally does.

Unfortunately one of those skipped checks was still needed when the item
being examined contained an *opaque* type – Rust’s way of saying “there is
a real type here but I’m keeping it hidden”.  In that situation the solver
sometimes concluded, incorrectly, that a method could never be called, so
it marked the vtable slot as “vacant”.  The rest of the compiler, however,
continued under the perfectly legal assumption that the slot *would* be
filled, and generated code that called through it.  Hence the jump to
address 0 and the crash.

Developers noticed the failure immediately: Glen De Cauwsemaecker’s
continuous-integration servers began dying the day Rust 1.98 was released.
He and others reduced his networking program to a two-hundred-line
test-case that reproduced the segfault in moments, making the underlying
problem easy to study.

A fix (#158993) was actually already waiting in the development branch.  
It tells the solver: “if you skipped a check earlier and you later discover
there’s an opaque type involved, *redo the analysis in the original stricter
mode*.”  With that second pass the solver realises the method really is
needed, supplies the correct address, and the program runs normally.

Nightly builds produced after the fix behaved correctly, which is why the
bug appeared only in the single stable release 1.98.0.  The Rust team
quickly back-ported the patch and issued Rust 1.98.1 less than two weeks
later.  Upgrading to 1.98.1 (or any later version) restores the missing
telephone numbers and eliminates the crash without any change to user
code.

In short: the compiler skipped a paperwork step, left a blank in a vital
lookup table, programs dialled the blank and crashed, and a patch restored
the missing information.

</div>

<h2 id="openai--gpt-5.4-2026-03-05">42. gpt-5.4-2026-03-05 · OpenAI API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

This bug was a compiler mistake in Rust 1.98.0, not a bug in the user’s application.

At a high level, the program used a Rust feature called **dynamic dispatch**: instead of knowing the exact type of a thing at compile time, the program stored “something that implements this interface” and called it through a pointer. To make that work, Rust builds a small lookup table called a **vtable**. A vtable contains the addresses of the real machine-code functions to call.

In this case, Rust 1.98.0 generated a broken vtable: one of the function slots that should have contained a valid address was filled with **zero**. Zero is not executable code. So when safe Rust code later said “call this method,” the compiled program looked up the method in the vtable, found zero, jumped to address 0, and crashed with a segmentation fault.

That is why the report described a “vacant vtable slot” and a crash at program counter 0. The source code itself was safe; the compiler emitted bad machine code.

Why did the compiler do that? The root cause was in Rust’s **trait solver**, the part of the compiler that proves facts like “this type satisfies these requirements.” During code generation, the compiler was deciding whether a particular method implementation could ever exist. Because of a bug, it sometimes concluded that the method was **impossible**, even though it was actually valid.

That wrong conclusion happened only in a narrow situation involving:
- trait objects and vtables,
- erased async/service-style code,
- opaque return types such as `impl Trait`,
- and a newer solver path already used on stable for some “impossible predicates” checks.

More specifically, the bug came from a change that introduced “rigid aliases,” an internal way for the compiler to avoid repeatedly re-normalizing certain hidden types. But when the solver encountered opaque types while working in a later compilation phase (“post-analysis” / codegen), it sometimes needed to **rerun the check in the original typing mode** to interpret those hidden types correctly. One fix for that behavior had already landed on nightly and beta, but it missed the 1.98.0 stable release window. Without that rerun, the solver could incorrectly treat a perfectly valid method as impossible, and the vtable builder then emitted an empty slot instead of a function pointer.

That explains the strange version behavior:
- **1.97.1**: worked
- **1.98.0**: miscompiled and crashed
- **nightly after mid-July 2026**: worked again

The issue was bisected to the change that introduced the rigid-alias behavior, and maintainers recognized that a later fix already existed: if opaque types are encountered in those codegen checks, the solver must rerun in the original mode. That fix was backported into **Rust 1.98.1**.

So the final fix was small in code but important in effect: it made the solver retry these cases under the correct interpretation of hidden types, which stopped it from wrongly declaring methods impossible. As a result, rustc once again generated proper vtables with real function addresses instead of null entries, and the crashes disappeared.

</div>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

Rust 1.98 contained a compiler bug that could turn valid, safe Rust code into a program that crashed at runtime. The affected code used a boxed asynchronous service: in effect, it stored an object behind an interface and later asked that interface to perform an operation.

An interface of this kind is implemented using a hidden table of function pointers, commonly called a *vtable*. The table contains housekeeping information—such as how to destroy the object—as well as pointers to the object’s actual methods. When the program calls a method through the interface, it looks up the appropriate pointer in the table and jumps to it.

In this case, Rust 1.98 generated a table whose first method entry was empty: it contained the null address, zero. The program then tried to call the service through that entry. The processor interpreted the zero as the address of a function and attempted to execute it, producing a segmentation fault. This is why the crash occurred at program counter zero, even though the source code itself contained no unsafe operation.

The compiler failed to generate the concrete implementation of the service method. It did generate the general, type-erased calling code—the code used to invoke “some service” without knowing its exact type—but it incorrectly concluded that the particular method needed for this table could never exist. It therefore marked the vtable slot as “vacant” rather than filling it with the real method pointer.

The error was especially easy to trigger with complicated combinations of generic types, boxed services, asynchronous functions, and traits. Asynchronous functions are represented internally using hidden future types, often called opaque types because their exact identity is concealed. The example also involved generic conditions such as “this type implements this interface, with an associated type satisfying another condition.” Most ordinary programs never produce precisely this combination, which explains why the failure appeared in one location while many other boxed services continued to work.

The underlying regression was related to changes in Rust’s newer trait solver, the compiler subsystem that decides whether type and interface requirements are satisfied. A prior change involving “rigid” type aliases altered how hidden types were handled. During a later compiler phase—after most type checking was complete—the solver sometimes examined an opaque type in the wrong typing context. It treated a valid requirement as impossible. Rust’s `impossible_predicates` logic then carried that mistaken conclusion into vtable generation.

The immediate correction was to retry the trait evaluation in the original typing context whenever this situation involved an opaque type. A change first made in pull request #158993 corrected the solver’s behavior. Because the regression had reached stable Rust in 1.98, the correction was backported in pull request #161555.

Rust 1.98.1, released on September 3, 2026, included that backport and a regression test based on this failure. The test ensures that a call through the generated interface works rather than using an empty vtable slot. Thus the application did not need to redesign its service abstraction or add unsafe code; upgrading from Rust 1.98.0 to Rust 1.98.1 fixed the compiler-generated vtable.

</div>

<h2 id="moonshot--kimi-k2.6">44. kimi-k2.6 · Moonshot API</h2>

<div class="explainer-text" markdown="1">

In the Rust programming language’s 1.98.0 release, the compiler shipped a rare but dangerous bug: it could build programs that crashed even though the source code was perfectly correct. Users reported segmentation faults—sudden halts caused by the program attempting to run code from a non-existent memory address. In a language whose central promise is preventing such crashes through its type system, this pointed squarely at the compiler itself. The compiler was generating broken binaries from safe, valid code.

The failure occurred during a pattern known as dynamic dispatch. When Rust code works with a generic capability—such as a “service” that can handle a network request—rather than a specific concrete type, the program relies on a lookup table called a vtable. This table tells the running program exactly where to find the real implementation of each method. In the buggy release, the compiler generated a vtable with a blank entry, filling the slot with a zero. When the program reached that step and jumped to memory address zero, the operating system terminated it instantly with a segmentation fault.

The root cause lay deep inside the compiler’s type-checking engine, specifically a newer component called the “next trait solver.” This solver was being used during an optimization pass that tries to prove certain code paths are impossible, allowing the compiler to remove dead weight. The bug triggered when the solver encountered opaque types—hidden concrete types behind interfaces like those returned by `async` functions or `impl Trait` annotations. During a late compilation stage, the solver failed to re-evaluate these hidden types in their proper original context. It mistakenly decided that a method implementation could never exist, so it omitted that method from the vtable, leaving the fatal blank slot.

The fix, developed in pull request #158993, forces the compiler to rerun its analysis in the correct context whenever it meets these opaque types during code generation. This prevents the solver from drawing false conclusions about what is impossible. Because the bug produced a near-guaranteed crash in valid, safe code, the patch was quickly backported to the stable release channel as version 1.98.1, arriving just two weeks after the issue was reported.

The incident is a reminder that even a language engineered for memory safety can suffer from compiler miscompilations. What matters is the response: the community identified the flaw, isolated it to a precise logic error in the type solver, and shipped a fix before the bug could affect a wider audience.
